as a reference, since we will soon generalize this argument to Riemannian manifolds.

Fix
\epsilon>0
and define the separation gap

\Delta_{\epsilon}\coloneqq\inf_{\theta\in\Theta:\ \|\theta-\theta_{\star}\|\geqslant\epsilon}\bigl(L(\theta)-L(\theta_{\star})\bigr),

so that
\Delta_{\epsilon}>0
by Assumption
B.2
(i). Consider the event

E_{m}\coloneqq\left\{\sup_{\theta\in\overline{B}(\theta_{\star},r)}\bigl|\hat{L}_{m}(\theta)-L(\theta)\bigr|\leqslant\frac{\Delta_{\epsilon}}{3}\right\}\cap\{\hat{\theta}_{m}\in\overline{B}(\theta_{\star},r)\}.

By the uniform law of large numbers on
\overline{B}(\theta_{\star},r)
and the localization
\mathbb{P}(\hat{\theta}_{m}\in\overline{B}(\theta_{\star},r))\to 1
,
we have
\mathbb{P}(E_{m})\to 1
.

On the event
E_{m}
, for any
\theta\in\overline{B}(\theta_{\star},r)
with
\|\theta-\theta_{\star}\|\geqslant\epsilon
we have

\hat{L}_{m}(\theta)\;\geqslant\;L(\theta)-\frac{\Delta_{\epsilon}}{3}\;\geqslant\;L(\theta_{\star})+\Delta_{\epsilon}-\frac{\Delta_{\epsilon}}{3}\;=\;L(\theta_{\star})+\frac{2\Delta_{\epsilon}}{3},

while

\hat{L}_{m}(\theta_{\star})\;\leqslant\;L(\theta_{\star})+\frac{\Delta_{\epsilon}}{3}.

Hence, on
E_{m}
,

\inf_{\theta\in\overline{B}(\theta_{\star},r):\ \|\theta-\theta_{\star}\|\geqslant\epsilon}\hat{L}_{m}(\theta)\;>\;\hat{L}_{m}(\theta_{\star}).

In particular, any minimizer of
\hat{L}_{m}
over
\overline{B}(\theta_{\star},r)
must lie in
B(\theta_{\star},\epsilon)
.
Since
\hat{\theta}_{m}\in\overline{B}(\theta_{\star},r)
on
E_{m}
and
\hat{\theta}_{m}
is (by assumption) an empirical minimizer, we conclude that

\|\hat{\theta}_{m}-\theta_{\star}\|<\epsilon
on
E_{m}
.
Therefore,

\mathbb{P}\big(\|\hat{\theta}_{m}-\theta_{\star}\|\geqslant\epsilon\big)\;\leqslant\;\mathbb{P}(E_{m}^{c})\ \to\ 0,

which proves
\hat{\theta}_{m}\to\theta_{\star}
in probability.
∎

Theorem B.4

(Euclidean
M
-estimator CLT)
.

Under
B.2
,

\sqrt{n}\,(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{\star})\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\ \mathcal{N}\!\big(0,\ H_{\star}^{-1}\Sigma_{\star}H_{\star}^{-1}\big).

Proof.

The argument follows standard
M
-estimation proofs (see, e.g.,
37
); we include it
as a reference, since we will soon generalize this argument to Riemannian manifolds.

By
Proposition

B.3
and
B.2
(i)–(ii), we have

\hat{\theta}_{m}\xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\theta_{\star}.

In particular, since
\hat{\theta}_{m}\in\overline{B}(\theta_{\star},r)
with probability tending to one by

B.2
(ii), all arguments below may be restricted to the event

\{\hat{\theta}_{m}\in\overline{B}(\theta_{\star},r)\}
.

On the event
\{\hat{\theta}_{m}\in\overline{B}(\theta_{\star},r)\}
, the first-order condition for the empirical minimizer gives

\nabla\hat{L}_{m}(\hat{\theta}_{m})=0.

Since
\ell(\,\cdot\,;z)
is
C^{2}
on
U
for
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
z
by
B.2
(iii), the map

\theta\mapsto\nabla\hat{L}_{m}(\theta)
is differentiable on
U
and we may apply the mean-value form of Taylor’s theorem:
there exists a point
\tilde{\theta}_{m}
on the line segment between
\theta_{\star}
and
\hat{\theta}_{m}
such that

0=\nabla\hat{L}_{m}(\hat{\theta}_{m})=\nabla\hat{L}_{m}(\theta_{\star})+\nabla^{2}\hat{L}_{m}(\tilde{\theta}_{m})\,(\hat{\theta}_{m}-\theta_{\star}).

(B.6)

Rearranging yields

\sqrt{m}\,(\hat{\theta}_{m}-\theta_{\star})=-\Big(\nabla^{2}\hat{L}_{m}(\tilde{\theta}_{m})\Big)^{-1}\,\sqrt{m}\,\nabla\hat{L}_{m}(\theta_{\star}),

(B.7)

on the event that
\nabla^{2}\hat{L}_{m}(\tilde{\theta}_{m})
is invertible.

Since
\tilde{\theta}_{m}
lies on the segment between
\theta_{\star}
and
\hat{\theta}_{m}
, we have

\tilde{\theta}_{m}\in\overline{B}(\theta_{\star},r)
whenever
\hat{\theta}_{m}\in\overline{B}(\theta_{\star},r)
.
Moreover, by consistency
\hat{\theta}_{m}\to\theta_{\star}
in probability, hence
\tilde{\theta}_{m}\to\theta_{\star}
in probability as well.
By the uniform Hessian convergence in
B.2
(v),

\sup_{\theta\in\overline{B}(\theta_{\star},r)}\big\|\nabla^{2}\hat{L}_{m}(\theta)-\nabla^{2}L(\theta)\big\|\ \xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\ 0,

and therefore

\nabla^{2}\hat{L}_{m}(\tilde{\theta}_{m})-\nabla^{2}L(\tilde{\theta}_{m})\ \xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\ 0.

Since
L
is twice differentiable at
\theta_{\star}
and
\tilde{\theta}_{m}\to\theta_{\star}
in probability, we also have

\nabla^{2}L(\tilde{\theta}_{m})\to\nabla^{2}L(\theta_{\star})=H_{\star}
in probability. Combining these gives

\nabla^{2}\hat{L}_{m}(\tilde{\theta}_{m})\ \xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\ H_{\star}.

(B.8)

By
B.2
(iv),
H_{\star}
is invertible, hence by continuity of matrix inversion,

\Big(\nabla^{2}\hat{L}_{m}(\tilde{\theta}_{m})\Big)^{-1}\ \xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\ H_{\star}^{-1}.

(B.9)

In particular,
\nabla^{2}\hat{L}_{m}(\tilde{\theta}_{m})
is invertible with probability tending to one.

By definition,

\nabla\hat{L}_{m}(\theta_{\star})=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\nabla\ell(\theta_{\star};Z_{i}).

Since
\theta_{\star}
is a minimizer of
L
and
L
is differentiable at
\theta_{\star}
, we have

\mathbb{E}[\nabla\ell(\theta_{\star};Z)]=\nabla L(\theta_{\star})=0
, hence the summands are mean-zero.
By
B.2
(iii),
\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla\ell(\theta_{\star};Z)\|^{2}]<\infty
, so the multivariate CLT yields

\sqrt{m}\,\nabla\hat{L}_{m}(\theta_{\star})\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\ \mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\star}).

(B.10)

Combining (
B.7
), (
B.9
), and (
B.10
), and applying Slutsky’s theorem,
we obtain

\sqrt{m}\,(\hat{\theta}_{m}-\theta_{\star})\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\ -\,H_{\star}^{-1}\,G,\qquad G\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\star}).

Since
-H_{\star}^{-1}G\sim\mathcal{N}(0,H_{\star}^{-1}\Sigma_{\star}H_{\star}^{-1})
, this proves the claim.
∎

B.4
M
-estimation on a Riemannian manifold

Let
(\mathcal{M},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle)
be a finite-dimensional
C^{2}
Riemannian manifold.
Let
(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{G})
be a measurable space and let
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m}
be i.i.d. with common law
\mathbb{P}
on
\mathcal{Z}
.
Let
f:\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable loss such that the expectations below are well-defined. Define

\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega)\coloneqq\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}f(\Omega;Z_{i}),\qquad F(\Omega)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[f(\Omega;Z)],\qquad\Omega\in\mathcal{M}.

An
M
-estimator
is a measurable map
\hat{\Omega}_{m}=\hat{\Omega}_{m}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m})
such that

\hat{\Omega}_{m}\in\argmin_{\Omega\in\mathcal{M}}\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega)
whenever the argmin set is nonempty.

Fix
\Omega_{\star}\in\mathcal{M}
. For
\epsilon>0
, define the tangent-space ball

B(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon)\coloneqq\{v\in T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}:\ \|v\|_{\Omega_{\star}}<\epsilon\},\qquad\overline{B}(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon)\coloneqq\{v\in T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}:\ \|v\|_{\Omega_{\star}}\leqslant\epsilon\}.

Let
\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}
denote the exponential map and

U=\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}\!\big(B(\Omega_{\star},\varepsilon_{0})\big)

denote a normal neighborhood.

Assumption B.5

(Riemannian
M
-estimation conditions)
.

There exist
\Omega_{\star}\in\mathcal{M}
, a normal neighborhood

U=\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}\!\big(B(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon_{0})\big)
, and
\epsilon^{\prime}\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
such that

\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}\!\big(\overline{B}(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon^{\prime})\big)\subseteq U
and:

(i)

(Identification and separation).

\Omega_{\star}
is the unique minimizer of
F
on
\mathcal{M}
and for every
\epsilon>0
,

\inf_{\Omega\in\mathcal{M}:\ d_{\mathcal{M}}(\Omega,\Omega_{\star})\geqslant\epsilon}\bigl(F(\Omega)-F(\Omega_{\star})\bigr)>0.

(ii)

(Uniform LLN on a compact set).

On the compact set
\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}\!\big(\overline{B}(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon^{\prime})\big)
, we have

\sup_{\Omega\in\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(\overline{B}(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon^{\prime}))}\bigl|\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega)-F(\Omega)\bigr|\ \xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\ 0,

and
\hat{\Omega}_{m}\in\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}\!\big(\overline{B}(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon^{\prime})\big)
with probability tending to one.

(iii)

(Local
C^{2}
smoothness and score moments).

For
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
z
, the map
\Omega\mapsto f(\Omega;z)
is
C^{2}
on
U
, and

\mathbb{E}\big[\|\mathrm{grad}\,f(\Omega_{\star};Z)\|_{\Omega_{\star}}^{2}\big]<\infty.

(iv)

(Nondegenerate minimizer).

The linear map
H_{\star}\coloneqq\mathrm{Hess}\,F(\Omega_{\star}):T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}\to T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}

is invertible.

(v)

(Uniform transported Hessian convergence on
\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(\overline{B}(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon^{\prime}))
).

For
\Omega\in\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}\!\big(\overline{B}(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon^{\prime})\big)
, define

\widetilde{H}_{m}(\Omega)\coloneqq\mathcal{P}_{\Omega\to\Omega_{\star}}\circ\mathrm{Hess}\,\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega)\circ\mathcal{P}_{\Omega_{\star}\to\Omega},\qquad\widetilde{H}(\Omega)\coloneqq\mathcal{P}_{\Omega\to\Omega_{\star}}\circ\mathrm{Hess}\,F(\Omega)\circ\mathcal{P}_{\Omega_{\star}\to\Omega}.

Then

\sup_{\Omega\in\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(\overline{B}(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon^{\prime}))}\big\|\widetilde{H}_{m}(\Omega)-\widetilde{H}(\Omega)\big\|\ \xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\ 0.

Define the covariance operator
\Sigma_{\star}
on
T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}
by

\langle u,\ \Sigma_{\star}v\rangle_{\Omega_{\star}}=\mathbb{E}\Big[\langle u,\ \mathrm{grad}\,f(\Omega_{\star};Z)\rangle_{\Omega_{\star}}\,\langle v,\ \mathrm{grad}\,f(\Omega_{\star};Z)\rangle_{\Omega_{\star}}\Big],\qquad u,v\in T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}.

Proposition B.6

(Riemannian
M
-estimator consistency)
.

Assume Assumption
B.5
(i)–(ii). Then

\hat{\Omega}_{m}\ \xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\ \Omega_{\star}.

Proof.

Fix
\epsilon>0
and define the separation gap

\Delta_{\epsilon}\coloneqq\inf_{\Omega\in\mathcal{M}:\ d_{\mathcal{M}}(\Omega,\Omega_{\star})\geqslant\epsilon}\bigl(F(\Omega)-F(\Omega_{\star})\bigr),

so that
\Delta_{\epsilon}>0
by Assumption
B.5
(i). Consider the event

E_{m}\coloneqq\left\{\sup_{\Omega\in\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(\overline{B}(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon^{\prime}))}\bigl|\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega)-F(\Omega)\bigr|\leqslant\frac{\Delta_{\epsilon}}{3}\right\}\cap\left\{\hat{\Omega}_{m}\in\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(\overline{B}(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon^{\prime}))\right\}.

By the uniform law of large numbers on
\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(\overline{B}(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon^{\prime}))
and the localization in
Assumption
B.5
(ii), we have
\mathbb{P}(E_{m})\to 1
.

On the event
E_{m}
, for any
\Omega\in\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(\overline{B}(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon^{\prime}))
with

d_{\mathcal{M}}(\Omega,\Omega_{\star})\geqslant\epsilon
we have

\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega)\;\geqslant\;F(\Omega)-\frac{\Delta_{\epsilon}}{3}\;\geqslant\;F(\Omega_{\star})+\Delta_{\epsilon}-\frac{\Delta_{\epsilon}}{3}\;=\;F(\Omega_{\star})+\frac{2\Delta_{\epsilon}}{3},

while

\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega_{\star})\;\leqslant\;F(\Omega_{\star})+\frac{\Delta_{\epsilon}}{3}.

Hence, on
E_{m}
,

\inf_{\Omega\in\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(\overline{B}(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon^{\prime})):\ d_{\mathcal{M}}(\Omega,\Omega_{\star})\geqslant\epsilon}\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega)\;>\;\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega_{\star}).

In particular, any minimizer of
\hat{F}_{m}
over
\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(\overline{B}(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon^{\prime}))
must lie in the metric ball

B_{d_{\mathcal{M}}}(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon)\coloneqq\{\Omega\in\mathcal{M}:\ d_{\mathcal{M}}(\Omega,\Omega_{\star})<\epsilon\}.

Since
\hat{\Omega}_{m}\in\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(\overline{B}(\Omega_{\star},\epsilon^{\prime}))
on
E_{m}
and
\hat{\Omega}_{m}
is (by definition) an empirical minimizer,
we conclude that
d_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{\Omega}_{m},\Omega_{\star})<\epsilon
on
E_{m}
. Therefore,

\mathbb{P}\big(d_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{\Omega}_{m},\Omega_{\star})\geqslant\epsilon\big)\;\leqslant\;\mathbb{P}(E_{m}^{c})\ \to\ 0,

which proves
\hat{\Omega}_{m}\to\Omega_{\star}
in probability.
∎

The following result is the full statement
of
Theorem

4.1
from the main text.

Theorem B.7

(Riemannian
M
-estimator CLT, full statement of
Theorem

4.1
)
.

Under
B.5
, we have

\sqrt{m}\,\log_{\Omega_{\star}}(\hat{\Omega}_{m})\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\ \mathcal{N}\!\big(0,\ H_{\star}^{-1}\Sigma_{\star}H_{\star}^{-1}\big),

where

H_{\star}\coloneqq\mathrm{Hess}\,F(\Omega_{\star}):T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}\to T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}

and

\Sigma_{\star}\coloneqq\mathrm{Var}\big(\mathrm{grad}\,f(\Omega_{\star};Z)\big)=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathrm{grad}\,f(\Omega_{\star};Z)\,\mathrm{grad}\,f(\Omega_{\star};Z)^{\top}\Big],

with
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{grad}\,f(\Omega_{\star};Z)]=\mathrm{grad}\,F(\Omega_{\star})=0
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

B.6
and
B.5
(i)–(ii), we have

\hat{\Omega}_{m}\xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\Omega_{\star}.

In particular, by
B.5
(ii), with probability tending to one we have

\hat{\Omega}_{m}\in U^{\prime}\coloneqq\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}\!\big(B_{\Omega_{\star}}(\epsilon^{\prime})\big)
, so that

v_{m}\coloneqq\log_{\Omega_{\star}}(\hat{\Omega}_{m})\in T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}

is well-defined and satisfies
v_{m}\to 0
in probability.

On the event
\{\hat{\Omega}_{m}\in U^{\prime}\}
, the first-order condition for an empirical minimizer gives

\mathrm{grad}\,\hat{F}_{m}(\hat{\Omega}_{m})=0.

Applying the transported gradient expansion (
B.5
) with
\Omega_{1}=\Omega_{\star}
,

\Omega_{2}=\hat{\Omega}_{m}=\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(v_{m})
, and
f=\hat{F}_{m}
, we obtain

0=\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\Omega}_{m}\to\Omega_{\star}}\big(\mathrm{grad}\,\hat{F}_{m}(\hat{\Omega}_{m})\big)=\mathrm{grad}\,\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega_{\star})+\mathrm{Hess}\,\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega_{\star})[v_{m}]+r_{m},

where
r_{m}=o(\|v_{m}\|_{\Omega_{\star}})
in probability as
m\to\infty
.
Rearranging yields

\sqrt{m}\,v_{m}=-\Big(\mathrm{Hess}\,\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega_{\star})\Big)^{-1}\,\sqrt{m}\,\mathrm{grad}\,\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega_{\star})-\Big(\mathrm{Hess}\,\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega_{\star})\Big)^{-1}\,\sqrt{m}\,r_{m},

(B.11)

on the event that
\mathrm{Hess}\,\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega_{\star})
is invertible.

By
B.5
(v) applied at
\Omega=\Omega_{\star}
(so that
\mathcal{P}_{\Omega_{\star}\to\Omega_{\star}}=\mathrm{Id}
),

\big\|\mathrm{Hess}\,\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega_{\star})-\mathrm{Hess}\,F(\Omega_{\star})\big\|=\big\|\widetilde{H}_{m}(\Omega_{\star})-\widetilde{H}(\Omega_{\star})\big\|\ \xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\ 0.

Hence,

\mathrm{Hess}\,\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega_{\star})\ \xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\ H_{\star}.

(B.12)

By
B.5
(iv),
H_{\star}
is invertible, and by continuity of inversion we have

\Big(\mathrm{Hess}\,\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega_{\star})\Big)^{-1}\ \xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\ H_{\star}^{-1}.

(B.13)

In particular,
\mathrm{Hess}\,\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega_{\star})
is invertible with probability tending to one.

By definition,

\mathrm{grad}\,\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega_{\star})=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{grad}\,f(\Omega_{\star};Z_{i}).

Since
\Omega_{\star}
is a minimizer of
F
and
F
is differentiable at
\Omega_{\star}
, we have

\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{grad}\,f(\Omega_{\star};Z)]=\mathrm{grad}\,F(\Omega_{\star})=0
.
By
B.5
(iii),
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathrm{grad}\,f(\Omega_{\star};Z)\|_{\Omega_{\star}}^{2}]<\infty
, so the multivariate CLT yields

\sqrt{m}\,\mathrm{grad}\,\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega_{\star})\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\ \mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\star}).

(B.14)

Since
r_{m}=o(\|v_{m}\|_{\Omega_{\star}})
in probability, we have

\frac{\|r_{m}\|_{\Omega_{\star}}}{\|v_{m}\|_{\Omega_{\star}}}\ \xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\ 0,

with the convention that the ratio is set to
0
on the event
\{v_{m}=0\}
.
Moreover, from (
B.11
) and (
B.13
) we have

\big(\mathrm{Hess}\,\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega_{\star})\big)^{-1}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
and

\sqrt{m}\,\mathrm{grad}\,\hat{F}_{m}(\Omega_{\star})=O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
, hence

\sqrt{m}\,\|v_{m}\|_{\Omega_{\star}}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

Consequently,

\sqrt{m}\,\|r_{m}\|_{\Omega_{\star}}=\big(\sqrt{m}\,\|v_{m}\|_{\Omega_{\star}}\big)\cdot\frac{\|r_{m}\|_{\Omega_{\star}}}{\|v_{m}\|_{\Omega_{\star}}}\ \xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\ 0,

and therefore

\sqrt{m}\,r_{m}\ \xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\ 0.

(B.15)

Combining (
B.11
), (
B.13
), (
B.14
), and (
B.15
),
and applying Slutsky’s theorem, we obtain

\sqrt{m}\,v_{m}=\sqrt{m}\,\log_{\Omega_{\star}}(\hat{\Omega}_{m})\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\ -\,H_{\star}^{-1}\,G,\qquad G\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\star}).

Since
-H_{\star}^{-1}G\sim\mathcal{N}(0,H_{\star}^{-1}\Sigma_{\star}H_{\star}^{-1})
, this proves the claim.
∎

Remark (Euclidean case as a special case).

If
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{p}
with the Euclidean metric, then
\exp_{\theta_{\star}}(v)=\theta_{\star}+v
,

\log_{\theta_{\star}}(\theta)=\theta-\theta_{\star}
, geodesics are line segments, and parallel transport is the identity.
In this case
\widetilde{H}_{n}(\theta)=\nabla^{2}\hat{L}_{n}(\theta)
.

Appendix C
Symmetry, Identifiability, and Quotient Geometry

This appendix formalizes how symmetry-induced non-identifiability in pretraining is handled in our analysis.

C.1
Quotients by group actions and local descriptor charts

Smooth group actions.

Let
G
be a Lie group acting smoothly on a smooth manifold
\mathcal{A}
.
We write the action as
(g,a)\mapsto g\cdot a
.
For
a\in\mathcal{A}
, the orbit is
[a]=\{g\cdot a:g\in G\}
and the stabilizer (isotropy subgroup) is

G_{a}=\{g\in G:g\cdot a=a\}
.
The orbit space (quotient set) is
\mathcal{A}/G=\{[a]:a\in\mathcal{A}\}
with the quotient topology, and
the canonical projection is denoted
\pi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}/G
,
\pi(a)=[a]
.

Regular neighborhoods and smooth quotients.

A smooth action is called
free
if
G_{a}=\{e\}
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
.
It is called
proper
if the map
G\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}
,

(g,a)\mapsto(a,g\cdot a)
is proper.
A sufficient condition for properness is that
G
is compact (e.g., an orthogonal group) or finite
(e.g., a permutation group).

If the action of
G
on
\mathcal{A}
is smooth, free, and proper on an open set
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
,
then the orbit space

\mathcal{B}\coloneqq\mathcal{U}/G

admits a unique smooth manifold structure such that the projection

\pi:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{B}
is a smooth submersion.
In this case,
\pi:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{B}
is a principal
G
-bundle.
If the action is proper but not free, one may restrict attention to a regular stratum on which the orbit type is constant;
on such a neighborhood the quotient is again a smooth manifold.
This is the regime implicitly used in the main text.

Local quotient charts via invariant descriptors.

Rather than working directly with the abstract quotient manifold
\mathcal{B}
,
we represent a local neighborhood of
\mathcal{B}
using an orbit-invariant
descriptor map
.
The following assumption makes this precise.

Assumption C.1

(Local quotient chart via an invariant descriptor)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be an open set on which the action of
G
is smooth, free, and proper,
and let
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{U}/G
with projection
\pi:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{B}
.
There exists a map
R:\mathcal{U}\to\mathbb{R}^{q}
such that:

(i)

(Orbit-constancy).

For all
a\in\mathcal{U}
and
g\in G
,

R(g\cdot a)=R(a).

(ii)

(Local chart for the quotient).

There exist open neighborhoods
V\subseteq\mathcal{B}
and
W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
and a
C^{k}
diffeomorphism
\bar{R}:V\to W
,
such that on
\pi^{-1}(V)
we have

R=\bar{R}\circ\pi.

Consequences.

Under Assumption
C.1
, the following hold.

(i)

Local orbit separation.

For
a,a^{\prime}\in\pi^{-1}(V)
,

R(a)=R(a^{\prime})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\pi(a)=\pi(a^{\prime})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad a^{\prime}\in[a].

(ii)

Descriptor manifold.

We identify the local quotient neighborhood
V\subseteq\mathcal{B}
with its descriptor coordinates

W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
via
\bar{R}
, and write

\mathcal{M}\coloneqq W.

Thus
\mathcal{M}
is a smooth manifold (indeed, an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{q}
) equipped with the induced Euclidean metric.

(iii)

Existence of smooth lifts.

Since
\pi:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{B}
is a principal bundle, there exists a smooth local section

\sigma:V\to\mathcal{U}
.
Defining

s\coloneqq\sigma\circ\bar{R}^{-1}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{U},

we obtain a
C^{k}

lift
satisfying
R(s(M))=M
for all
M\in\mathcal{M}
.

(iv)

Well-defined induced objectives.

Any
G
-invariant function on
\mathcal{U}
induces a well-defined function on
\mathcal{M}

by evaluation at any representative in
R^{-1}(M)\cap\pi^{-1}(V)
.

C.2
Vector-bundle viewpoint: quotient-level features and the coordinate feature map
\phi(x,M)

This subsection formalizes how equivariant representative-level features induce intrinsic
quotient-level features, and how coordinate feature maps arise from choosing local lifts.

C.2.1
Setup: principal bundle and equivariant features

Let
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q_{0}}
be an open set on which a Lie group
G
acts smoothly, freely, and properly,
and let
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{U}/G
with projection
\pi:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{B}
.
Fix a feature dimension
p\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and let
\rho:G\to O(p)
be a smooth group homomorphism.
Let
\psi:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{U}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
be measurable in
x
and
C^{k}
in its second argument.

We assume the orthogonal equivariance condition

\psi(x,g\cdot A)=\rho(g)\,\psi(x,A),\qquad x\in\mathcal{X},\ A\in\mathcal{U},\ g\in G.

C.2.2
Associated vector bundle and intrinsic feature section

Define an equivalence relation on
\mathcal{U}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}
by

(A,v)\sim(g\cdot A,\rho(g)v),\qquad g\in G.

The associated rank-
p
vector bundle over
\mathcal{B}
is

\mathcal{E}\coloneqq(\mathcal{U}\times\mathbb{R}^{p})/\sim,

with projection
\pi_{\mathcal{E}}([A,v])=[A]
.
The Euclidean inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{p}
descends to a well-defined fiberwise inner product on
\mathcal{E}
.

For each
x\in\mathcal{X}
, define the intrinsic feature section

\Phi_{x}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{E},\qquad\Phi_{x}([A])\coloneqq[A,\psi(x,A)].

Proposition C.2

(Well-definedness and smoothness)
.

Under the equivariance assumption above,
\Phi_{x}
is well-defined.
If
A\mapsto\psi(x,A)
is
C^{k}
on
\mathcal{U}
, then
\Phi_{x}
is a
C^{k}
section of
\mathcal{E}
.

Proof.

If
A^{\prime}=g\cdot A
, then by equivariance,

[A^{\prime},\psi(x,A^{\prime})]=[g\cdot A,\rho(g)\psi(x,A)]=[A,\psi(x,A)],

so
\Phi_{x}
depends only on the orbit.
Smoothness follows by expressing
\Phi_{x}
in local trivializations induced by smooth local sections
of the principal bundle
\pi:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{B}
.
∎

C.2.3
Descriptor coordinates and the coordinate feature map

Let
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
be the descriptor manifold provided by
Assumption
C.1
, and let

s:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{U}
be the associated
C^{k}
lift, i.e.,
R(s(\Omega))=\Omega
for all
\Omega
in a local neighborhood.

Coordinate feature map.

Define

\phi:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{p},\qquad\phi(x,M)\coloneqq\psi(x,s(M)).

Lemma C.3

(Differentiability of
\phi
)
.

If
A\mapsto\psi(x,A)
is
C^{k}
and
s
is
C^{k}
, then for each fixed
x\in\mathcal{X}

the map
M\mapsto\phi(x,M)
is
C^{k}
on
\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

Fix
x\in\mathcal{X}
and define
\psi_{x}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
by
\psi_{x}(A)\coloneqq\psi(x,A)
.
By assumption,
\psi_{x}
is a
C^{k}
map on
\mathcal{U}
, and
s
is
C^{k}
on
\mathcal{M}
.
Hence
\phi_{x}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
given by

\phi_{x}(M)\coloneqq\phi(x,M)=\psi_{x}\bigl(s(M)\bigr)

is the composition
\phi_{x}=\psi_{x}\circ s
of two
C^{k}
maps between smooth manifolds, and is therefore
C^{k}
.
∎

Gauge transformations.

If
s^{\prime}
is another
C^{k}
lift on
\mathcal{M}
, then for each
M\in\mathcal{M}
there exists
a unique
g(M)\in G
such that
s^{\prime}(M)=g(M)\cdot s(M)
.
The map
M\mapsto g(M)
is
C^{k}
.

Lemma C.4

(Gauge transformation rule)
.

If
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
are induced by lifts
s
and
s^{\prime}
respectively, then

\phi^{\prime}(x,M)=\rho(g(M))\,\phi(x,M),\qquad x\in\mathcal{X},\ M\in\mathcal{M}.

Proof.

By equivariance,

\phi^{\prime}(x,M)=\psi(x,s^{\prime}(M))=\psi(x,g(M)\cdot s(M))=\rho(g(M))\,\phi(x,M).

∎

Intrinsic meaning.

The intrinsic object is the bundle section
\Phi_{x}
.
Choosing a lift
s
identifies each fiber with
\mathbb{R}^{p}
and yields the coordinate representation
\phi(x,M)
.
Different lifts correspond to orthogonal changes of coordinates.

C.2.4
Orbit-invariance of minimum-norm OLS

The proof of
Lemma

4.2
in the main text follows directly
from orthogonal equivariance and is given below for completeness.

Proof of
Lemma

4.2
.

Fix a downstream dataset
D_{\mathrm{down}}^{(n)}=\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
and write

Y\coloneqq(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
For any
w\in\mathbb{R}^{q_{0}}
, define the design matrix
\Psi_{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
by

(\Psi_{w})_{i,:}\coloneqq\psi(x_{i},w)^{\top}.

The minimum Euclidean norm solution of the OLS problem is given by

\hat{\theta}_{w}=\Psi_{w}^{+}Y,

where
(\cdot)^{+}
denotes the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse.

By the orthogonal equivariance condition (
4.4
), for any
g\in G
and each
i
,

\psi(x_{i},g\cdot w)^{\top}=(\rho(g)\psi(x_{i},w))^{\top}=\psi(x_{i},w)^{\top}\rho(g)^{\top}.

Therefore,

\Psi_{g\cdot w}=\Psi_{w}\,\rho(g)^{\top}.

For any matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
and any orthogonal matrix
Q\in O(p)
,

(MQ)^{+}=Q^{\top}M^{+}.

Applying this identity with
M=\Psi_{w}
and
Q=\rho(g)^{\top}
yields

\hat{\theta}_{g\cdot w}=\Psi_{g\cdot w}^{+}Y=(\Psi_{w}\rho(g)^{\top})^{+}Y=\rho(g)\Psi_{w}^{+}Y=\rho(g)\hat{\theta}_{w}.

For any
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{g\cdot w}(x)

\displaystyle=\langle\hat{\theta}_{g\cdot w},\psi(x,g\cdot w)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\rho(g)\hat{\theta}_{w},\rho(g)\psi(x,w)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\hat{\theta}_{w},\psi(x,w)\rangle

\displaystyle=\hat{f}_{w}(x),

where we used the orthogonality of
\rho(g)
in the third equality.
This shows that the minimum-norm downstream predictor depends on
w

only through its orbit
[w]
.
∎

Appendix D
Proof of Proposition
5.2

Recall the downstream regression model in Equation (
3.1
)

Y=f_{\star}(X)+\varepsilon,\qquad X\sim\mu_{\mathrm{down}},\qquad\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon\mid X]=0,\qquad\sigma^{2}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{2}\mid X]<\infty.

Let
\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
be i.i.d. copies of
(X,Y)
and write

D_{\mathrm{down}}^{(n)}=\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
for the labeled sample and

X_{1:n}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
for the downstream design.
Let
(X_{\mathrm{new}},Y_{\mathrm{new}})
be an independent copy of
(X,Y)
.
Define
\varepsilon_{i}\coloneqq y_{i}-f_{\star}(x_{i})
for
i\in[n]
and

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{new}}\coloneqq Y_{\mathrm{new}}-f_{\star}(X_{\mathrm{new}})
.

Manifold-valued feature parameters and quenched conditioning.

In the main text, the feature parameter is learned in pre-training:
\Omega=\hat{\Omega}_{m}(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)})
, and
\Omega
may take values on
a Riemannian manifold
\mathcal{M}
. All identities below are deterministic once
(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n})
is fixed, because conditioning on
D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}

freezes
\Omega
, and conditioning on
X_{1:n}
freezes the empirical projection operator
\Pi_{\Omega,n}
.
This viewpoint isolates the downstream label noise and the fresh test pair randomness, without averaging over pre-training and downstream design.

D.1
Empirical projection notation

Recall the empirical inner product

\langle g,h\rangle_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(x_{i})h(x_{i}).

Let
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}=\{T_{\Omega}\theta:\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\}
denote the induced linear class and
\Pi_{\Omega,n}
be the Moore–Penrose empirical least-squares map defined in Appendix
A
(Definition
A.7
).
We will invoke the following properties (Lemma
A.8
and
Lemma
A.9
): for any
g
with finite evaluations on
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
,

1.

\Pi_{\Omega,n}g\in\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
and
\langle g-\Pi_{\Omega,n}g,\,h\rangle_{n}=0
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
;

2.

\mathrm{Ev}_{n}(\Pi_{\Omega,n}h)=\mathrm{Ev}_{n}(h)
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}

(equivalently,
\|h-\Pi_{\Omega,n}h\|_{n}=0
).

When
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{n}
is non-degenerate on
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
(equivalently,
\mathrm{Ev}_{n}
is injective on

\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
), these properties imply
\Pi_{\Omega,n}h=h
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
, i.e.
\Pi_{\Omega,n}
is the unique
empirical orthogonal projector onto
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
.

OLS as empirical projection.

Let
\hat{\theta}_{\Omega,n}
be the minimum-norm OLS solution and set
\hat{f}_{\Omega,n}=T_{\Omega}\hat{\theta}_{\Omega,n}
.
Write the sample-value vectors

y_{1:n}\coloneqq(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})^{\top},\qquad\varepsilon_{1:n}\coloneqq(\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n})^{\top},\qquad f_{\star,1:n}\coloneqq\bigl(f_{\star}(x_{1}),\dots,f_{\star}(x_{n})\bigr)^{\top},

so that
y_{1:n}=f_{\star,1:n}+\varepsilon_{1:n}
.
By Lemma
A.11
,

\hat{f}_{\Omega,n}=\Pi_{\Omega,n}\,\mathrm{lift}_{n}(y_{1:n}).

By linearity of
\Pi_{\Omega,n}
and
y_{1:n}=f_{\star,1:n}+\varepsilon_{1:n}
,

\hat{f}_{\Omega,n}=\Pi_{\Omega,n}f_{\star}+\Pi_{\Omega,n}\,\mathrm{lift}_{n}(\varepsilon_{1:n}).

(D.1)

For brevity, we will write
\Pi_{\Omega,n}\varepsilon
instead of
\Pi_{\Omega,n}\,\mathrm{lift}_{n}(\varepsilon_{1:n})
, with the
understanding that this means applying
\Pi_{\Omega,n}
to any measurable representative whose evaluations
on
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
equal
\varepsilon_{1:n}
.

D.2
Population decomposition and orthogonality

Recall the population projector
\Pi_{\Omega}=T_{\Omega}\Sigma(\Omega)^{+}T_{\Omega}^{\mathrm{adj}}
onto
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
in
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
and define

e_{\Omega}\coloneqq(I-\Pi_{\Omega})f_{\star},\qquad\mathrm{Rep}(\Omega)\coloneqq\|e_{\Omega}\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}^{2}.

Since
\Pi_{\Omega}
is the
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
-orthogonal projector onto
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
, we have

e_{\Omega}\perp\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}\quad\text{in }L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}}),\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad\langle e_{\Omega},h\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}=0\ \ \forall h\in\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}.

We will use the decomposition

f_{\star}=\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star}+e_{\Omega}.

(D.2)

D.3
Exact risk decomposition conditional on
(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n})

We now restate and prove
Proposition

5.2
.
See
5.2

Proof.

Start from the identity
Y_{\mathrm{new}}=f_{\star}(X_{\mathrm{new}})+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{new}}
and write

Y_{\mathrm{new}}-\hat{f}_{\Omega,n}(X_{\mathrm{new}})=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{new}}+\bigl(f_{\star}-\hat{f}_{\Omega,n}\bigr)(X_{\mathrm{new}}).

Using (
D.1
), we have

\hat{f}_{\Omega,n}=\Pi_{\Omega,n}f_{\star}+\Pi_{\Omega,n}\varepsilon.

Substituting and then adding and subtracting
\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star}
yields

\displaystyle Y_{\mathrm{new}}-\hat{f}_{\Omega,n}(X_{\mathrm{new}})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{new}}+\bigl(f_{\star}-\Pi_{\Omega,n}f_{\star}-\Pi_{\Omega,n}\varepsilon\bigr)(X_{\mathrm{new}})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{new}}+\bigl((f_{\star}-\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star})-(\Pi_{\Omega,n}f_{\star}-\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star})-\Pi_{\Omega,n}\varepsilon\bigr)(X_{\mathrm{new}})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{new}}+\bigl(e_{\Omega}-(\Pi_{\Omega,n}f_{\star}-\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star})-\Pi_{\Omega,n}\varepsilon\bigr)(X_{\mathrm{new}}),

(D.3)

where in the last step we used (
D.2
).

Square (
D.3
) and take conditional expectation given
(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n})
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(Y_{\mathrm{new}}-\hat{f}_{\Omega,n}(X_{\mathrm{new}})\bigr)^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\varepsilon_{\mathrm{new}}^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\!\left[\Bigl(e_{\Omega}-(\Pi_{\Omega,n}f_{\star}-\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star})-\Pi_{\Omega,n}\varepsilon\Bigr)(X_{\mathrm{new}})^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+2\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\varepsilon_{\mathrm{new}}\Bigl(e_{\Omega}-(\Pi_{\Omega,n}f_{\star}-\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star})-\Pi_{\Omega,n}\varepsilon\Bigr)(X_{\mathrm{new}})\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right].

(D.4)

Cross term with
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{new}}
.

Condition on
(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n},X_{\mathrm{new}},\varepsilon_{1:n})
.
The bracketed term in (
D.4
) evaluated at
X_{\mathrm{new}}
is measurable with respect to

(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n},X_{\mathrm{new}},\varepsilon_{1:n})
, while
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{\mathrm{new}}\mid X_{\mathrm{new}}]=0
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\varepsilon_{\mathrm{new}}\Bigl(e_{\Omega}-(\Pi_{\Omega,n}f_{\star}-\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star})-\Pi_{\Omega,n}\varepsilon\Bigr)(X_{\mathrm{new}})\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\mathbb{E}\!\left[\varepsilon_{\mathrm{new}}\Bigl(e_{\Omega}-(\Pi_{\Omega,n}f_{\star}-\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star})-\Pi_{\Omega,n}\varepsilon\Bigr)(X_{\mathrm{new}})\,\middle|\,X_{\mathrm{new}},\varepsilon_{1:n}\right]\bigg|D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\mathbb{E}\!\left[\varepsilon_{\mathrm{new}}\,\middle|\,X_{\mathrm{new}},\varepsilon_{1:n}\right]\Bigl(e_{\Omega}-(\Pi_{\Omega,n}f_{\star}-\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star})-\Pi_{\Omega,n}\varepsilon\Bigr)(X_{\mathrm{new}})\bigg|D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\Bigg]=0.

Since
(X_{\mathrm{new}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{new}})
is an independent copy of
(X,\varepsilon)
and is independent of
(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n},\varepsilon_{1:n})
,

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\varepsilon_{\mathrm{new}}^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right]=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{2}]=\sigma^{2}.

Set

g\coloneqq\Pi_{\Omega,n}f_{\star}-\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star}\in\mathcal{H}_{\Omega},\qquad u\coloneqq\Pi_{\Omega,n}\varepsilon\in\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}.

Then pointwise,

(e_{\Omega}-g-u)^{2}=e_{\Omega}^{2}+g^{2}+u^{2}-2e_{\Omega}g-2e_{\Omega}u+2gu.

Evaluating at
X_{\mathrm{new}}
and taking
\mathbb{E}[\cdot\mid D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}]
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[(e_{\Omega}-g-u)(X_{\mathrm{new}})^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[e_{\Omega}(X_{\mathrm{new}})^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right]+\mathbb{E}\!\left[g(X_{\mathrm{new}})^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-2\,\langle e_{\Omega},g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}-2\,\langle e_{\Omega},u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}

\displaystyle\quad+2\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[g(X_{\mathrm{new}})u(X_{\mathrm{new}})\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\!\left[u(X_{\mathrm{new}})^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right].

(D.5)

Orthogonality kills the
e_{\Omega}
cross terms.

Since
g,u\in\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
and
e_{\Omega}\perp\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
in
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
, we have

\langle e_{\Omega},g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}=\langle e_{\Omega},u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}=0.

The remaining cross term averages to zero.

Condition on
(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n},X_{\mathrm{new}})
.
Given
(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n})
, the function
g
is deterministic (because
\Omega
is
D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}
-measurable and
\Pi_{\Omega,n}
depends only on
(\Omega,X_{1:n})
),
while
u=\Pi_{\Omega,n}\varepsilon
is linear in the noise vector
\varepsilon_{1:n}
.
Using
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{1:n}\mid X_{1:n}]=0
and independence of
D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}
from the downstream noise, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\!\left[u(X_{\mathrm{new}})\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n},X_{\mathrm{new}}\right]=0,

and hence

\mathbb{E}\!\left[g(X_{\mathrm{new}})u(X_{\mathrm{new}})\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n},X_{\mathrm{new}}\right]=0.

Therefore,

\mathbb{E}\!\left[g(X_{\mathrm{new}})u(X_{\mathrm{new}})\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right]=0.

Conclusion.

Using
\mathbb{E}[e_{\Omega}(X_{\mathrm{new}})^{2}]=\|e_{\Omega}\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}^{2}=\mathrm{Rep}(\Omega)
in (
D.5
)
and substituting into (
D.4
) yields (
5.1
).
∎

D.4
Well-posedness specialization

Corollary D.1

(Well-posedness implies
\Pi_{\Omega,n}f_{\star}-\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star}=\Pi_{\Omega,n}f_{\Omega}
)
.

Assume
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{n}
is non-degenerate on
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
(equivalently,
\mathrm{Ev}_{n}
is injective on
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
).
Then
\Pi_{\Omega,n}h=h
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
, and therefore

\displaystyle\Pi_{\Omega,n}f_{\star}-\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star}=\Pi_{\Omega,n}e_{\Omega}.

Consequently, (
5.1
) reduces to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(Y_{\mathrm{new}}-\hat{f}_{\Omega,n}(X_{\mathrm{new}})\bigr)^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{2}]+\mathrm{Rep}(\Omega)+\mathbb{E}\!\left[(\Pi_{\Omega,n}e_{\Omega})(X_{\mathrm{new}})^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\!\left[(\Pi_{\Omega,n}\varepsilon)(X_{\mathrm{new}})^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right].

Proof.

Under the stated condition,
\Pi_{\Omega,n}h=h
holds for all
h\in\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
.
Since
\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star}\in\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
and
f_{\star}=\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star}+e_{\Omega}
, we have

\Pi_{\Omega,n}f_{\star}=\Pi_{\Omega,n}(\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star}+e_{\Omega})=\Pi_{\Omega,n}(\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star})+\Pi_{\Omega,n}e_{\Omega}=\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star}+\Pi_{\Omega,n}e_{\Omega},

which implies
\Pi_{\Omega,n}f_{\star}-\Pi_{\Omega}f_{\star}=\Pi_{\Omega,n}e_{\Omega}
.
Substituting this identity into (
5.1
) gives the claimed formula.
∎

Bridge to the Riemannian log-map CLT.

In the main text we set
\Omega=\hat{\Omega}_{m}(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)})\in\mathcal{M}
.
The conditional risk is

R(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n})=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(Y_{\mathrm{new}}-\hat{f}_{\hat{\Omega}_{m},n}(X_{\mathrm{new}})\bigr)^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right],

which is obtained by substituting
\Omega=\hat{\Omega}_{m}
into (
5.1
).
The Riemannian structure enters when analyzing the fluctuations of
\hat{\Omega}_{m}
around
\Omega_{\star}
through the log map:
under the assumptions stated in the main text,

\sqrt{m}\,\log_{\Omega_{\star}}(\hat{\Omega}_{m})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}Z,\qquad Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,V)\ \text{in }T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}.

Since the decomposition (
5.1
) holds conditionally on
(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n})
for each
m,n
,
one can combine this log-map CLT with a delta-method argument for the map
\Omega\mapsto R(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n})

in a normal neighborhood of
\Omega_{\star}
, without taking expectations over
D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}
.

Appendix E
Proof of Theorem
5.3

E.1
Downstream estimation terms

This section studies the two downstream
estimation
terms that appear in the exact conditional risk decomposition
(Proposition
5.2
) where
both
the pre-training and downstream sample sizes diverge with an asymptotic constant rate
\frac{m}{n}\to\alpha\in(0,\infty)
.
Throughout we work on a coupled probability space supporting three mutually independent objects:

(i)

An i.i.d. pre-training sequence
(Z^{\mathrm{pre}}_{j})_{j\geqslant 1}
,

(ii)

An i.i.d. downstream sequence
(X_{i},\varepsilon_{i})_{i\geqslant 1}
with
X_{i}\sim\mu_{\mathrm{down}}
and
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{i}\mid X_{i}]=0
,

(iii)

An independent test covariate
X_{\mathrm{new}}\sim\mu_{\mathrm{down}}
(and an independent noise
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{new}}
)

For each
m
, define the pre-training dataset
D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}\coloneqq(Z^{\mathrm{pre}}_{1},\dots,Z^{\mathrm{pre}}_{m})
and the feature parameter

\Omega_{m}\coloneqq\hat{\Omega}_{m}\!\big(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}\big).

We allow the pretraining sample size to depend on the downstream sample size:
Fix
\alpha\in(0,\infty)
. For each
n
let
m\coloneqq\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor
.
Then
m\to\infty
and
m/n\to\alpha
as
n\to\infty
.

Conditioning convention.

All conditional expectations in this appendix are taken
given

D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}

(and, when appropriate, given
X_{1:n}
as well).
Once we condition on
D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}
,
\Omega_{m}
is fixed, and the downstream sample

(X_{i},\varepsilon_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
remains i.i.d. and independent of
D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}
.

E.1.1
Estimation terms and the compatible regime

The conditional risk at stage
n
is

R(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(Y_{\mathrm{new}}-\hat{f}_{\Omega_{m},n}(X_{\mathrm{new}})\bigr)^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right].

Recall from Proposition
5.2
that the two downstream estimation terms are

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(\Pi_{\Omega_{m},n}\varepsilon\bigr)(X_{\mathrm{new}})^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right],

(E.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Leakage}_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(\Pi_{\Omega_{m},n}f_{\star}-\Pi_{\Omega_{m}}f_{\star}\bigr)(X_{\mathrm{new}})^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right].

(E.2)

Here
\Pi_{\Omega_{m}}
is the population
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
projector onto
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega_{m}}
and

\Pi_{\Omega_{m},n}
is the canonical empirical projector from Appendix
A

(Definition
A.7
).

Define the population residual
e_{\Omega}\coloneqq(I-\Pi_{\Omega})f_{\star}
, so that
e_{\Omega}\perp\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
in
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
. Under the well-posedness condition in
E.3
(stated below), one has

\Pi_{\Omega_{m},n}f_{\star}-\Pi_{\Omega_{m}}f_{\star}=\Pi_{\Omega_{m},n}e_{\Omega_{m}},

(E.3)

and hence
\mathrm{Leakage}_{n}
reduces to a residual-leakage term.

Compatible limit.

In the main theorem we work in the
compatible
regime where
f_{\star}\in\mathcal{H}_{\Omega_{\star}}
for the limit feature parameter

\Omega_{\star}
, equivalently

e_{\Omega_{\star}}=(I-\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}})f_{\star}=0.

(E.4)

As a consequence, once
\Omega_{m}\to\Omega_{\star}
in probability, we will show that the signal estimation term
\mathrm{Leakage}_{n}
vanishes at the scale
n
.

E.1.2
Setup and standing regularity (triangular array)

For each
n
, define the population and empirical covariances

\displaystyle\Sigma_{m}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Sigma(\Omega_{m})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\!\left[\phi(X,\Omega_{m})\phi(X,\Omega_{m})^{\top}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{m}\right],

\displaystyle\qquad\Sigma_{m,n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Sigma_{n}(\Omega_{m})\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi(X_{i},\Omega_{m})\phi(X_{i},\Omega_{m})^{\top},

where the expectation in
\Sigma_{m}
is over
X\sim\mu_{\mathrm{down}}
and all quantities are random through
D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}

(and, for
\Sigma_{m,n}
, also through
X_{1:n}
).

Assumption E.1

(Stable rank and eigengap along
(\Omega_{m})
)
.

There exist an integer
r\in\{0,1,\dots,p\}
and constants
\kappa,K<\infty
such that

\mathbb{P}\!\Big(\rank(\Sigma_{m})=r,\ \lambda_{r}(\Sigma_{m})\geqslant\kappa,\ \|\Sigma_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant K\Big)\ \to\ 1\qquad\text{as }m\to\infty,

where
\lambda_{r}(\Sigma)
denotes the smallest positive eigenvalue (the condition is vacuous if
r=0
).

Assumption E.2

(Local-uniform moment bounds near
\Omega_{\star}
)
.

There exist
\delta>0
, a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
\Omega_{\star}
in
\mathcal{M}
, and constants

C_{\phi},C_{e}<\infty
such that

\sup_{\Omega\in\mathcal{U}}\ \mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\phi(X,\Omega)\|^{4+\delta}\right]\leqslant C_{\phi}.

Moreover, for the signal term,

\sup_{\Omega\in\mathcal{U}}\ \mathbb{E}\!\left[e_{\Omega}(X)^{2}\,\|\phi(X,\Omega)\|^{2+\delta}\right]\leqslant C_{e}.

Assumption E.3

(Well-posedness for identifying the leakage term)
.

With probability tending to
1
as
m\to\infty
(under the joint law of
(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n})
),
the empirical inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{n}
is non-degenerate on
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega_{m}}

(equivalently, the evaluation map on
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
is injective on
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega_{m}}
).
On this event,
\Pi_{\Omega_{m},n}h=h
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}_{\Omega_{m}}
, and hence (
E.3
) holds (Appendix
D.4
).

Assumption E.4

(Local
C^{1}
regularity of the feature map in
\Omega
with moment control)
.

Work in normal coordinates on a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
\Omega_{\star}
.
Assume that for
\mu_{\mathrm{down}}
-a.e.
x
, the map
\Omega\mapsto\phi(x,\Omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is differentiable on
\mathcal{U}
.
Moreover, there exist
\delta>0
(the same
\delta
as in
E.2
) and a constant
C_{\partial\phi}<\infty
such that

\sup_{\Omega\in\mathcal{U}}\ \mathbb{E}\!\Big[\|D_{\Omega}\phi(X,\Omega)\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{4+\delta}\Big]\leqslant C_{\partial\phi},

(E.5)

where
D_{\Omega}\phi(x,\Omega):T_{\Omega}\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
denotes the derivative in normal coordinates and

\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}
is the operator norm.

Differentiability of the population projector.

In the next results we verify that the population
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
-orthogonal projector

\Pi_{\Omega}
onto the downstream hypothesis class
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
is Fréchet differentiable
in
\Omega
near
\Omega_{\star}
. The key input is differentiability of the pseudoinverse on the
stable-rank region (Assumption
E.1
).

Lemma E.5

(Differentiability of the pseudoinverse on the stable-rank region)
.

Let
A\succcurlyeq 0
be symmetric with
\rank(A)=r
and
\lambda_{r}(A)\geqslant\kappa>0
.
Then the restriction of the Moore–Penrose map to the stable-rank region

\mathcal{R}_{r,\kappa}\coloneqq\{B\succcurlyeq 0:\ B=B^{\top},\ \rank(B)=r,\ \lambda_{r}(B)\geqslant\kappa\}

is Fréchet differentiable at
A
(in operator norm). Its derivative in direction
H
is

D(A^{+})[H]=-\,A^{+}HA^{+}+A^{+2}H(I-AA^{+})+(I-A^{+}A)HA^{+2}.

(E.6)

In particular, there exists a constant
C_{\kappa}<\infty
depending only on
\kappa
such that

\|D(A^{+})[H]\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant C_{\kappa}\,\|H\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Proof.

This is a standard result in perturbation theory. For the proof see
14
.
∎

Proposition E.6

(Differentiability of
\Omega\mapsto\Pi_{\Omega}
on a constant-rank region)
.

Fix
\Omega_{\star}\in\mathcal{M}
. Assume
E.2
and
E.4
.
Assume moreover that there exist an open neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
\Omega_{\star}
and constants

r\in\{0,1,\dots,p\}
and
\kappa>0
such that for all
\Omega\in\mathcal{U}
,

\rank\!\bigl(\Sigma(\Omega)\bigr)=r,\qquad\lambda_{r}\!\bigl(\Sigma(\Omega)\bigr)\geqslant\kappa\quad(\text{vacuous if }r=0).

Then the map
\Omega\mapsto\Pi_{\Omega}
is Fréchet differentiable at
\Omega_{\star}

as a map into
\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}}))
(operator norm). In particular, for

v\in T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}
,

\bigl\|\Pi_{\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(v)}-\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}-D\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}[v]\bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o(\|v\|)\qquad\text{as }v\to 0.

In the region of differentiability, the derivative can be obtained by

\displaystyle D\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}[v]\;g=(DT_{\Omega_{\star}}[v])\,\Sigma_{\star}^{+}\,c_{g}^{\star}+T_{\Omega_{\star}}\,\bigl(D\Sigma(\Omega_{\star})^{+}[v]\bigr)\,c_{g}^{\star}+T_{\Omega_{\star}}\,\Sigma_{\star}^{+}\,Dc_{g}(\Omega_{\star})[v].

(E.7)

Proof.

Fix
v\in T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}
and write
\Omega(v)\coloneqq\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(v)
.
Throughout we work for
\|v\|
small enough so that
\Omega(v)\in\mathcal{U}
.

By
E.2
,
\sup_{\Omega\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{E}\|\phi(X,\Omega)\|^{2}<\infty
, hence

T_{\Omega}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
is bounded for each
\Omega\in\mathcal{U}
and

\sup_{\Omega\in\mathcal{U}}\|T_{\Omega}\|_{\mathrm{op}}<\infty
where we define

\|T_{\Omega}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\coloneqq\sup_{\|\theta\|=1}\|T_{\Omega}\theta\|_{L^{2}_{\mathrm{down}}}.

We show that
\Omega\mapsto T_{\Omega}
is Fréchet differentiable at
\Omega_{\star}
as a map into

\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{p},L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}}))
equipped with
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}
, with derivative

(DT_{\Omega_{\star}}[v]\theta)(x)=\theta^{\top}D_{\Omega}\phi(x,\Omega_{\star})[v].

By
E.4
, for
\mu_{\mathrm{down}}
-a.e.
x
the map
\Omega\mapsto\phi(x,\Omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is Fréchet differentiable at
\Omega_{\star}
.
Equivalently, there exists a remainder term
r(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that for all
v
sufficiently small,

\phi(x,\Omega(v))=\phi(x,\Omega_{\star})+D_{\Omega}\phi(x,\Omega_{\star})[v]+r(x,v),\qquad\frac{\|r(x,v)\|}{\|v\|}\to 0\ \ \text{as }v\to 0.

(E.8)

To upgrade (
E.8
) to an
L^{2}
statement we need an integrable domination.
By the moment domination part of
E.2
(together with
E.4
),
there exists
\delta>0
and an integrable envelope
G\in L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
such that for all
\|v\|\leqslant\delta
,

\left\|\frac{\phi(x,\Omega(v))-\phi(x,\Omega_{\star})-D_{\Omega}\phi(x,\Omega_{\star})[v]}{\|v\|}\right\|\leqslant G(x)\qquad\text{for $\mu_{\mathrm{down}}$-a.e.\ $x$}.

(E.9)

(Concretely, one may take
G(x)=\sup_{\Omega\in\mathcal{U}}\|D_{\Omega}\phi(x,\Omega)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
on a sufficiently small neighborhood

\mathcal{U}
of
\Omega_{\star}
, and
E.2
ensures
G\in L^{2}
.)

Combining (
E.8
)–(
E.9
) and dominated convergence yields

\left\|\phi(\cdot,\Omega(v))-\phi(\cdot,\Omega_{\star})-D_{\Omega}\phi(\cdot,\Omega_{\star})[v]\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}};\mathbb{R}^{p})}=o(\|v\|).

(E.10)

For any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
with
\|\theta\|_{2}=1
, using (
E.10
) and Cauchy–Schwarz gives

\displaystyle\bigl\|\bigl(T_{\Omega(v)}-T_{\Omega_{\star}}-DT_{\Omega_{\star}}[v]\bigr)\theta\bigr\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\theta^{\top}\!\left(\phi(\cdot,\Omega(v))-\phi(\cdot,\Omega_{\star})-D_{\Omega}\phi(\cdot,\Omega_{\star})[v]\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\|\phi(\cdot,\Omega(v))-\phi(\cdot,\Omega_{\star})-D_{\Omega}\phi(\cdot,\Omega_{\star})[v]\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}};\mathbb{R}^{p})}

\displaystyle=o(\|v\|).

Taking the supremum over
\|\theta\|_{2}=1
yields

\|T_{\Omega(v)}-T_{\Omega_{\star}}-DT_{\Omega_{\star}}[v]\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o(\|v\|),

which is the claimed Fréchet differentiability in operator norm.

Fix
g\in L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
and define

c_{g}(\Omega)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[\phi(X,\Omega)\,g(X)]\in\mathbb{R}^{p}.

By Cauchy–Schwarz and
E.2
,

\sup_{\Omega\in\mathcal{U}}\|c_{g}(\Omega)\|\lesssim\|g\|_{L_{\mathrm{down}}^{2}}
since

\|c_{g}(\Omega)\|\leqslant(\mathbb{E}[\phi(X,\Omega)^{2}])^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{E}[g(X)^{2}])^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Next, Dominated convergence again gives

\|c_{g}(\Omega(v))-c_{g}(\Omega_{\star})-Dc_{g}(\Omega_{\star})[v]\|=o(\|v\|),\qquad Dc_{g}(\Omega_{\star})[v]=\mathbb{E}[D_{\Omega}\phi(X,\Omega_{\star})[v]\;g(X)].

Similarly,
\Omega\mapsto\Sigma(\Omega)=\mathbb{E}[\phi(X,\Omega)\phi(X,\Omega)^{\top}]
is Fréchet differentiable at

\Omega_{\star}
, with

\|\Sigma(\Omega(v))-\Sigma(\Omega_{\star})-D\Sigma(\Omega_{\star})[v]\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o(\|v\|),

where

D\Sigma(\Omega_{\star})[v]=\mathbb{E}\!\Big[D_{\Omega}\phi(X,\Omega_{\star})[v]\;\phi_{\star}(X)^{\top}+\phi_{\star}(X)\;D_{\Omega}\phi(X,\Omega_{\star})[v]^{\top}\Big].

By the stable-rank hypothesis on
\mathcal{U}
, for all
\Omega\in\mathcal{U}
we have

\rank(\Sigma(\Omega))=r
and
\lambda_{r}(\Sigma(\Omega))\geqslant\kappa
(vacuous if
r=0
), hence

\sup_{\Omega\in\mathcal{U}}\|\Sigma(\Omega)^{+}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant 1/\kappa
.
Applying Lemma
E.5
at
A=\Sigma_{\star}
gives

\|\Sigma(\Omega(v))^{+}-\Sigma_{\star}^{+}-D\Sigma(\Omega_{\star})^{+}[v]\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o(\|v\|),

where
D\Sigma(\Omega_{\star})^{+}[v]\coloneqq D(\Sigma_{\star}^{+})[D\Sigma(\Omega_{\star})[v]]
.

For any
g\in L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
, we have

\Pi_{\Omega}g=T_{\Omega}\,\Sigma(\Omega)^{+}\,c_{g}(\Omega).

Write
\Delta T\coloneqq T_{\Omega(v)}-T_{\Omega_{\star}}
,
\Delta\Sigma^{+}\coloneqq\Sigma(\Omega(v))^{+}-\Sigma_{\star}^{+}
,
and
\Delta c\coloneqq c_{g}(\Omega(v))-c_{g}(\Omega_{\star})
. Then

\Pi_{\Omega(v)}g-\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}g=(\Delta T)\,\Sigma(\Omega(v))^{+}\,c_{g}(\Omega(v))+T_{\Omega_{\star}}\,(\Delta\Sigma^{+})\,c_{g}(\Omega(v))+T_{\Omega_{\star}}\,\Sigma_{\star}^{+}\,(\Delta c)

which can be written as

\Pi_{\Omega(v)}g-\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}g-D\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}[v]\,g=R_{1}(v)g+R_{2}(v)g+R_{3}(v)g,

where

\displaystyle D\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}[v]\;g

\displaystyle=(DT_{\Omega_{\star}}[v])\,\Sigma_{\star}^{+}\,c_{g}^{\star}+T_{\Omega_{\star}}\,\bigl(D\Sigma(\Omega_{\star})^{+}[v]\bigr)\,c_{g}^{\star}+T_{\Omega_{\star}}\,\Sigma_{\star}^{+}\,Dc_{g}(\Omega_{\star})[v],

\displaystyle R_{1}(v)g

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigl(\Delta T-DT_{\Omega_{\star}}[v]\bigr)\,\Sigma(\Omega(v))^{+}\,c_{g}(\Omega(v)),

\displaystyle R_{2}(v)g

\displaystyle\coloneqq T_{\Omega_{\star}}\,\bigl(\Delta\Sigma^{+}-D\Sigma(\Omega_{\star})^{+}[v]\bigr)\,c_{g}(\Omega(v)),

\displaystyle R_{3}(v)g

\displaystyle\coloneqq T_{\Omega_{\star}}\,\Sigma_{\star}^{+}\,\bigl(\Delta c-Dc_{g}(\Omega_{\star})[v]\bigr).

Next, because of the differentiability of
T
,
\Sigma^{+}
, and
c_{g}(\Omega)
, we have
\|\Delta T-DT_{\Omega_{\star}}[v]\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o(\|v\|)
and

\|\Delta\Sigma^{+}-D\Sigma(\Omega_{\star})^{+}[v]\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o(\|v\|)
, while

\|\Delta c-Dc_{g}(\Omega_{\star})[v]\|=o(\|v\|)
.
Moreover, on
\mathcal{U}
we have uniform bounds

\sup_{\Omega\in\mathcal{U}}\|T_{\Omega}\|_{\mathrm{op}}<\infty
,

\sup_{\Omega\in\mathcal{U}}\|\Sigma(\Omega)^{+}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant 1/\kappa
,
and
\sup_{\Omega\in\mathcal{U}}\|c_{g}(\Omega)\|\lesssim\|g\|_{L^{2}}
.
Therefore,

\|R_{1}(v)g\|_{L^{2}}+\|R_{2}(v)g\|_{L^{2}}+\|R_{3}(v)g\|_{L^{2}}=o(\|v\|)\,\|g\|_{L^{2}}.

Taking the supremum over
\|g\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant 1
yields

\|\Pi_{\Omega(v)}-\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}-D\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}[v]\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o(\|v\|),

which is exactly Fréchet differentiability at
\Omega_{\star}
in operator norm.
∎

Corollary E.7

(Compatible limit implies vanishing residual)
.

Assume there exists
\Omega_{\star}
such that
\Omega_{m}\to\Omega_{\star}
in probability and

f_{\star}\in\mathcal{H}_{\Omega_{\star}}
(equivalently,
\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}f_{\star}=f_{\star}
in
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
).
Assume moreover that the population projector map
\Omega\mapsto\Pi_{\Omega}
is continuous at
\Omega_{\star}
in operator norm, i.e.

\|\Pi_{\Omega}-\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\to 0\quad\text{as }\Omega\to\Omega_{\star},

where
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}
denotes the operator norm on
\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}}))
.
Then

\|e_{\Omega_{m}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}=\|(I-\Pi_{\Omega_{m}})f_{\star}\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}\to 0\quad\text{in probability.}

Proof.

Since
f_{\star}\in\mathcal{H}_{\Omega_{\star}}
, we have
\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}f_{\star}=f_{\star}
, hence

e_{\Omega_{m}}=(I-\Pi_{\Omega_{m}})f_{\star}=(\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}-\Pi_{\Omega_{m}})f_{\star}.

Therefore,

\|e_{\Omega_{m}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}\leqslant\|\Pi_{\Omega_{m}}-\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\;\|f_{\star}\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}.

By the assumed continuity of
\Omega\mapsto\Pi_{\Omega}
at
\Omega_{\star}
and the convergence
\Omega_{m}\to\Omega_{\star}
in probability,
we have
\|\Pi_{\Omega_{m}}-\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\to 0
in probability, which implies the claim.
∎

Remark E.8
.

In view of Proposition
E.6
, if
\Omega\mapsto\Pi_{\Omega}
is Fréchet differentiable at
\Omega_{\star}
in operator norm
(e.g. on the rank-stable stratum with a
C^{1}
feature map in
\Omega
and the local-uniform moment bounds),
then the operator-norm continuity required above holds automatically.

E.1.3
Two perturbation lemmas for pseudoinverses

Lemma E.9

(Empirical span is contained in the population span)
.

Fix
m
and condition on
D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}
.
Assume
\mathbb{E}[\|\phi(X,\Omega_{m})\|^{2}\mid D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}]<\infty
, so that
\Sigma_{m}
is well-defined.
Let
S\coloneqq\mathrm{Im}(\Sigma_{m})
. Then
\phi(X,\Omega_{m})\in S
almost surely.
In particular,
\mathrm{Im}(\Sigma_{m,n})\subseteq S
and
\rank(\Sigma_{m,n})\leqslant\rank(\Sigma_{m})
almost surely.

Proof.

Let
K\coloneqq\ker(\Sigma_{m})
and let
v
be any unit vector in
K
. Then

0=v^{\top}\Sigma_{m}v=\mathbb{E}\!\left[(v^{\top}\phi(X,\Omega_{m}))^{2}\right],

so
v^{\top}\phi(X,\Omega_{m})=0
almost surely. Hence
\phi(X,\Omega_{m})\in K^{\perp}=\mathrm{Im}(\Sigma_{m})
almost surely.
Applying the same argument to each
X_{i}
yields the claims for
\Sigma_{m,n}
.
∎

Lemma E.10

(Rank stability and Lipschitz control for the pseudoinverse)
.

Assume
E.2
. Fix
m
and condition on
D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}
.
Let

\mathcal{R}_{m}\coloneqq\Bigl\{\rank(\Sigma_{m})=r,\ \lambda_{r}(\Sigma_{m})\geqslant\kappa\Bigr\}.

On the event
\mathcal{R}_{m}\cap\{\|\Sigma_{m,n}-\Sigma_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\kappa/2\}
, almost surely:

(i)

\rank(\Sigma_{m,n})=\rank(\Sigma_{m})=r
and
\mathrm{Im}(\Sigma_{m,n})=\mathrm{Im}(\Sigma_{m})
;

(ii)

\|\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant 2/\kappa
;

(iii)

\|\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}-\Sigma_{m}^{+}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\frac{2}{\kappa^{2}}\,\|\Sigma_{m,n}-\Sigma_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

Proof.

Assume
\mathcal{R}_{m}
and
\|\Sigma_{m,n}-\Sigma_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\kappa/2
.
By Weyl’s inequality,

\lambda_{r}(\Sigma_{m,n})\geqslant\lambda_{r}(\Sigma_{m})-\|\Sigma_{m,n}-\Sigma_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geqslant\kappa/2,

so
\rank(\Sigma_{m,n})\geqslant r
. On the other hand,
Lemma

E.9
gives

\mathrm{Im}(\Sigma_{m,n})\subseteq\mathrm{Im}(\Sigma_{m})
almost surely, hence
\rank(\Sigma_{m,n})\leqslant r
.
Therefore
\rank(\Sigma_{m,n})=r
, and since
\mathrm{Im}(\Sigma_{m,n})\subseteq\mathrm{Im}(\Sigma_{m})
with equal dimension,

\mathrm{Im}(\Sigma_{m,n})=\mathrm{Im}(\Sigma_{m})=:S.

This proves (i). For (ii), on
S
the matrix
\Sigma_{m,n}
is invertible and

\lambda_{\min}(\Sigma_{m,n}|_{S})=\lambda_{r}(\Sigma_{m,n})\geqslant\kappa/2
, so
\|(\Sigma_{m,n}|_{S})^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant 2/\kappa
.
Since
\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}
coincides with
(\Sigma_{m,n}|_{S})^{-1}
on
S
and is
0
on
S^{\perp}
, (ii) follows.
For (iii), on
S
,

(\Sigma_{m,n}|_{S})^{-1}-(\Sigma_{m}|_{S})^{-1}=(\Sigma_{m,n}|_{S})^{-1}\,(\Sigma_{m}|_{S}-\Sigma_{m,n}|_{S})\,(\Sigma_{m}|_{S})^{-1}.

Taking operator norms and using
\|(\Sigma_{m,n}|_{S})^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant 2/\kappa
and
\|(\Sigma_{m}|_{S})^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant 1/\kappa

yields the stated bound, which extends to
\mathbb{R}^{p}
since both pseudoinverses vanish on
S^{\perp}
.
∎

Lemma E.11

(Operator-norm consistency of
\Sigma_{m,n}
(fixed
p
))
.

Assume
E.2
and let
n\to\infty
.
Then

\|\Sigma_{m,n}-\Sigma_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0.

Proof.

The claim is a standard consequence of matrix Law of Large Numbers for sample covariance matrices (in fixed dimension) under the moment condition in
E.2
; see, e.g.,
38
.
Fix
\varepsilon>0
.
Condition on
D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}
, so that
\Omega_{m}
and
n
are fixed and

X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are i.i.d. with law
\mu_{\mathrm{down}}
.

Write
\Sigma_{m,n}-\Sigma_{m}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{m,i}
with

U_{m,i}\coloneqq\phi(X_{i},\Omega_{m})\phi(X_{i},\Omega_{m})^{\top}-\mathbb{E}\!\left[\phi(X,\Omega_{m})\phi(X,\Omega_{m})^{\top}\right].

For each entry
(j,k)
, let
U_{m,i}^{(jk)}
be the
(j,k)
entry of
U_{m,i}
.
Then
\mathbb{E}[U_{m,i}^{(jk)}\mid D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}]=0
and, by
E.2
,
there is a constant
C<\infty
such that on the high-probability event
\{\Omega_{m}\in\mathcal{U}\}
,

\mathbb{E}\!\left[(U_{m,1}^{(jk)})^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}\right]\leqslant C.

Hence, still on
\{\Omega_{m}\in\mathcal{U}\}
, Chebyshev gives

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{m,i}^{(jk)}\right|>\frac{\varepsilon}{p}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}\right)\leqslant\frac{C\,p^{2}}{n\,\varepsilon^{2}}.

Taking a union bound over the
p^{2}
entries yields

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\|\Sigma_{m,n}-\Sigma_{m}\|_{\mathrm{F}}>\varepsilon\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}\right)\leqslant\frac{C\,p^{4}}{n\,\varepsilon^{2}}\quad\text{on }\{\Omega_{m}\in\mathcal{U}\}.

Since
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{F}}
, the same bound holds for the operator norm.
Now uncondition:

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\|\Sigma_{m,n}-\Sigma_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}>\varepsilon\right)\leqslant\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{m}\notin\mathcal{U})+\mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{C\,p^{4}}{n\,\varepsilon^{2}}\right].

Because
\Omega_{m}\to\Omega_{\star}
in probability,
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{m}\notin\mathcal{U})\to 0
for any neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
\Omega_{\star}
.
Also,
n\to\infty
in probability implies
1/n\to 0
in probability, and since
0\leqslant 1/n\leqslant 1
,
we have
\mathbb{E}[1/n]\to 0
. Therefore the right-hand side tends to
0
, proving the claim.
∎

Lemma E.12

(Effective dimension stabilizes)
.

Assume
E.1
. Then

d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\Omega_{m})=\tr(\Sigma_{m}\Sigma_{m}^{+})=\rank(\Sigma_{m})=\rank(\Sigma(\Omega_{\star}))=d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\Omega_{\star})

with probability tending to one as
m\to\infty
.

Proof.

By
E.1
,
\rank(\Sigma_{m})=r
with probability tending to one.
Since
\Omega_{m}\to\Omega_{\star}
in probability, and on the event in
E.1
the eigengap

\lambda_{r}(\Sigma_{m})\geqslant\kappa
prevents rank changes in a neighborhood.
In particular,
\rank(\Sigma(\Omega_{\star}))=r
and hence
d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\Omega_{m})=d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\Omega_{\star})=r
with probability tending to one.
∎

E.1.4
Noise estimation term

Lemma E.13

(Closed form for the noise term)
.

For each
m
,

\mathrm{Var}_{n}=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\,\tr\!\bigl(\Sigma_{m}\,\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}\bigr).

Proof.

Fix
n
and condition on
(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n},X_{\mathrm{new}})
.
By Definition
A.7
(Appendix
A
),

\Pi_{\Omega_{m},n}\varepsilon=T_{\Omega_{m}}\hat{\theta}_{\varepsilon},\qquad\hat{\theta}_{\varepsilon}=\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}\,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\,\phi(X_{i},\Omega_{m}).

Let
g_{m}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\phi(X_{i},\Omega_{m})
, so
\hat{\theta}_{\varepsilon}=\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}g_{m}
and

(\Pi_{\Omega_{m},n}\varepsilon)(X_{\mathrm{new}})=\phi(X_{\mathrm{new}},\Omega_{m})^{\top}\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}g_{m}.

Since
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{i}\mid X_{i}]=0
and
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\mid X_{i}]=\sigma^{2}
,

\mathbb{E}[g_{m}g_{m}^{\top}\mid D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}]=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\mid X_{i}]\phi(X_{i},\Omega_{m})\phi(X_{i},\Omega_{m})^{\top}=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\,\Sigma_{m,n}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{n}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\phi(X_{\mathrm{new}},\Omega_{m})^{\top}\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}g_{m}g_{m}^{\top}\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}\phi(X_{\mathrm{new}},\Omega_{m})\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n},X_{\mathrm{new}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\,\phi(X_{\mathrm{new}},\Omega_{m})^{\top}\Sigma_{{m},n}^{+}\phi(X_{\mathrm{new}},\Omega_{m}),

using
\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}\Sigma_{m,n}\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}=\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}
.
Taking conditional expectation over
X_{\mathrm{new}}
yields

\mathrm{Var}_{m}=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\,\tr\!\bigl(\Sigma_{m}\,\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}\bigr).

∎

Proposition E.14

(Noise estimation term)
.

Assume
E.1
and
E.2
.
Then, as
n\to\infty
,

n\,\mathrm{Var}_{n}\ \xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\ \sigma^{2}\,d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\Omega_{\star}),\qquad d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\Omega)\coloneqq\tr\!\bigl(\Sigma(\Omega)\Sigma(\Omega)^{+}\bigr).

Proof.

By
Lemma

E.13
,

n\,\mathrm{Var}_{m}=\sigma^{2}\,\tr\!\bigl(\Sigma_{m}\,\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}\bigr).

Let
A_{m}\coloneqq\tr(\Sigma_{m}\Sigma_{m,n}^{+})
and
A_{m}^{\star}\coloneqq\tr(\Sigma_{m}\Sigma_{m}^{+})=d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\Omega_{m})
.
Define
\mathcal{R}_{m}\coloneqq\{\rank(\Sigma_{m})=r,\ \lambda_{r}(\Sigma_{m})\geqslant\kappa,\ \|\Sigma_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant K\}
.
On the event
\mathcal{R}_{m}\cap\mathcal{E}_{m}
where
\mathcal{E}_{m}\coloneqq\{\|\Sigma_{m,n}-\Sigma_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\kappa/2\}
,

Lemma

E.10
(iii) implies

|A_{m}-A_{m}^{\star}|=\big|\tr\big(\Sigma_{m}(\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}-\Sigma_{m}^{+})\big)\big|\leqslant\rank(\Sigma_{m})\,\|\Sigma_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\|\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}-\Sigma_{m}^{+}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\frac{2rK}{\kappa^{2}}\,\|\Sigma_{m,n}-\Sigma_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

By
E.1
,
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{R}_{m})\to 1
. By
Lemma

E.11
,
\|\Sigma_{m,n}-\Sigma_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\to 0

in probability, hence
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{m})\to 1
and
A_{m}-A_{m}^{\star}\to 0
in probability. Multiplying by
\sigma^{2}
gives

n\,\mathrm{Var}_{m}-\sigma^{2}\,d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\Omega_{m})\to 0\quad\text{in probability.}

Finally,
Lemma

E.12
gives
d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\Omega_{m})=d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\Omega_{\star})
with probability tending to one, which yields

n\,\mathrm{Var}_{m}\to\sigma^{2}d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\Omega_{\star})
in probability.
∎

E.1.5
Leakage Estimation Term

Under
E.3
, (
E.3
) yields

\mathrm{Leakage}_{n}=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(\Pi_{\Omega_{m},n}e_{\Omega_{m}}\bigr)(X_{\mathrm{new}})^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n}\right].

Define the (random) population matrix

\Sigma_{e,m}\coloneqq\Sigma_{e}(\Omega_{m})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\!\left[e_{\Omega_{m}}(X)^{2}\,\phi(X,\Omega_{m})\phi(X,\Omega_{m})^{\top}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}\right],\qquad X\sim\mu_{\mathrm{down}}.

Lemma E.15

(Vanishing signal covariance in the compatible limit)
.

Assume
E.2
and
E.4
. Then

\tr(\Sigma_{e,m})\xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\ 0.

Proof.

We have

\tr(\Sigma_{e,m})=\mathbb{E}\!\left[e_{\Omega_{m}}(X)^{2}\,\|\phi(X,\Omega_{m})\|^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}\right].

By Hölder with exponents
\frac{2+\delta}{\delta}
and
\frac{2+\delta}{2}
,

\tr(\Sigma_{e,m})\leqslant\Bigl(\mathbb{E}[e_{\Omega_{m}}(X)^{2}\mid D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}]\Bigr)^{\frac{\delta}{2+\delta}}\Bigl(\mathbb{E}[\|\phi(X,\Omega_{m})\|^{2+\delta}\mid D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}]\Bigr)^{\frac{2}{2+\delta}}.

By
Corollary

E.7
,
\mathbb{E}[e_{\Omega_{m}}(X)^{2}]\to 0
in probability.
By
E.2
and
\|\phi\|^{2+\delta}\leqslant 1+\|\phi\|^{4+\delta}
, the second factor is bounded in probability.
Therefore
\tr(\Sigma_{e,m})\to 0
in probability.
∎

Proposition E.16

(Leakage estimation term)
.

Assume
E.1
,
E.2
,
E.3
, and
E.4
.
Then

n\,\mathrm{Leakage}_{n}\ \xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }\ 0\qquad\text{as }n\to\infty.

Proof.

Fix
n
and condition on
(D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)},X_{1:n})
. Under
E.3
, we may write

\mathrm{Leakage}_{n}=\Pi_{\Omega_{m},n}e_{\Omega_{m}}=T_{\Omega_{m}}\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}\,g_{m},\qquad g_{m}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{\Omega_{m}}(X_{i})\phi(X_{i},\Omega_{m}).

Thus

(\Pi_{\Omega_{m},n}e_{\Omega_{m}})(X_{\mathrm{new}})=\phi(X_{\mathrm{new}},\Omega_{m})^{\top}\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}g_{m},

and taking conditional expectation over
X_{\mathrm{new}}
yields the quadratic form

\mathrm{Leakage}_{m}=g_{m}^{\top}\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}\Sigma_{m}\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}\,g_{m}.

Therefore

n\,\mathrm{Leakage}_{m}=(\sqrt{n}\,g_{m})^{\top}B_{m}(\sqrt{n}\,g_{m}),\qquad B_{m}\coloneqq\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}\Sigma_{m}\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}.

\sqrt{n_{m}}\,g_{m}\to 0
in probability.

Condition on
D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}
.
Then the random vectors

W_{m,i}\coloneqq e_{\Omega_{m}}(X_{i})\,\phi(X_{i},\Omega_{m}),\qquad i=1,\dots,n_{m},

are i.i.d. with conditional mean zero, by orthogonality of
e_{\Omega_{m}}
to

\mathcal{H}_{\Omega_{m}}
.
Moreover,

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\sqrt{n_{m}}\,g_{m}\|^{2}\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}\right]=\tr(\Sigma_{e,m}).

Hence, for any
\varepsilon>0
, Markov’s inequality yields

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\|\sqrt{n_{m}}\,g_{m}\|>\varepsilon\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}\right)\leqslant\frac{\tr(\Sigma_{e,m})}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

Fix
\delta>0
and define the event

\mathcal{A}_{m}\coloneqq\bigl\{\tr(\Sigma_{e,m})\leqslant\delta\bigr\}.

Then

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\|\sqrt{n_{m}}\,g_{m}\|>\varepsilon\right)\leqslant\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{m}^{c})+\mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{m}}\,\mathbb{P}\!\left(\|\sqrt{n_{m}}\,g_{m}\|>\varepsilon\,\middle|\,D_{\mathrm{pre}}^{(m)}\right)\right]\leqslant\mathbb{P}\!\left(\tr(\Sigma_{e,m})>\delta\right)+\frac{\delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

By Lemma
E.15
,
\tr(\Sigma_{e,m})\to 0
in probability, hence the first term
vanishes as
m\to\infty
. Since
\delta>0
is arbitrary, letting
\delta\downarrow 0
yields

\sqrt{n_{m}}\,g_{m}\xrightarrow{\ \mathbb{P}\ }0

under the joint law.

On the event
\mathcal{R}_{m}\cap\mathcal{E}_{m}
defined in the proof of
Proposition

E.14
,

Lemma

E.10
(ii) and
\|\Sigma_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant K
imply

\|B_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\|\Sigma_{m,n}^{+}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\|\Sigma_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\frac{4K}{\kappa^{2}}.

Since
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{R}_{m})\to 1
by
E.1
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{m})\to 1
by
Lemma

E.11
,
we conclude
\|B_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
.

Finally, we have the following

0\leqslant n\,\mathrm{Leakage}_{m}\leqslant\|B_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\|\sqrt{n}\,g_{m}\|^{2}.

The right-hand side converges to
0
in probability, hence
n\,\mathrm{Leakage}_{m}\to 0
in probability.
∎

E.2
Pretraining fluctuations

This appendix studies the effect of pre-training randomness on the
representation term

\mathrm{Rep}(\Omega)\coloneqq\|(I-\Pi_{\Omega})f_{\star}\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}^{2},

where, for any descriptor value
\Omega
in the descriptor manifold
\mathcal{M}
,
\Pi_{\Omega}
denotes the

L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
-orthogonal projector onto the downstream hypothesis class
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
.

E.2.1
Main statements

Write

e_{\Omega}\coloneqq(I-\Pi_{\Omega})f_{\star},\qquad\mathrm{Rep}(\Omega)=\|e_{\Omega}\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}^{2}.

Let
\Omega_{\star}\in\mathcal{M}
denote the population limit descriptor. In the compatible regime,

\mathrm{Rep}(\Omega_{\star})=0,\qquad\text{equivalently }e_{\Omega_{\star}}=0.

(E.11)

The leading behavior of
\mathrm{Rep}(\Omega_{m})
is quadratic in the local estimation error of
\Omega_{m}
around
\Omega_{\star}
.
The next proposition gives the distributional limit at the
1/m
scale.

Proposition E.17

(Representation term: distributional limit in the compatible case)
.

Assume the pretraining data satisfies
B.5

Z_{m}\coloneqq\sqrt{m}\,\log_{\Omega_{\star}}(\Omega_{m})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}Z,\qquad Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,V),

in
T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}
. Moreover, assume Assumption
E.4
so that
\Omega\mapsto\Pi_{\Omega}
be Fréchet differentiable at
\Omega_{\star}
in normal coordinates, and define the bounded linear map

\mathcal{L}:T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}\to L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}}),\qquad\mathcal{L}(v)\coloneqq-D\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}[v]\,f_{\star}.

Then

m\,\mathrm{Rep}(\Omega_{m})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\|\mathcal{L}(Z)\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}^{2}.

The next proposition records the
1/m
expansion in expectation. Since the limiting random variable has finite mean,
it suffices to assume uniform integrability of
\{m\,\mathrm{Rep}(\Omega_{m})\}
to upgrade distributional convergence to convergence
of expectations.

E.2.2
Setup and a sufficient uniform integrability condition

We work on a normal neighborhood of
\Omega_{\star}
in
\mathcal{M}
(
Definition

B.1
).
Define

v_{m}\coloneqq\log_{\Omega_{\star}}(\Omega_{m})\in T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M},\qquad Z_{m}\coloneqq\sqrt{m}\,v_{m}.

By the
Theorem

B.7
,
Z_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}Z
in
T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}
for
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,V)
.

E.2.3
Linearization in normal coordinates

Lemma E.18

(Deterministic first-order remainder bound)
.

Assume (
E.11
) and Assumption
E.4
. Then there exists a function

\omega:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
with
\omega(t)\to 0
as
t\to 0
such that for all
v\in T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}

in a neighborhood of
0
,

\bigl\|e_{\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(v)}-\mathcal{L}(v)\bigr\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}\leqslant\omega(\|v\|)\,\|v\|.

Proof.

Write
\Pi_{\star}\coloneqq\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}
. By compatibility,
\Pi_{\star}f_{\star}=f_{\star}
in
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
, hence

e_{\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(v)}=(I-\Pi_{\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(v)})f_{\star}=-(\Pi_{\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(v)}-\Pi_{\star})f_{\star}.

By Lemma
E.6
,

\Pi_{\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(v)}-\Pi_{\star}=D\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}[v]+R(v),\qquad\|R(v)\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o(\|v\|).

Define

\omega(t)\coloneqq\sup_{\|v\|\leqslant t,\,v\neq 0}\frac{\|R(v)\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{\|v\|},

so that
\omega(t)\to 0
as
t\to 0
. Then

e_{\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(v)}=-D\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}[v]f_{\star}-R(v)f_{\star}=\mathcal{L}(v)+r(v),\qquad r(v)\coloneqq-R(v)f_{\star}.

Moreover,

\|r(v)\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}\leqslant\|R(v)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|f_{\star}\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}\leqslant\omega(\|v\|)\,\|v\|\,\|f_{\star}\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}.

Absorb the constant
\|f_{\star}\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}
into
\omega
.
∎

E.2.4
Distributional limit of the representation term

By Lemma
E.18
,

e_{\Omega_{m}}=e_{\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(v_{m})}=\mathcal{L}(v_{m})+r(v_{m}),\qquad\|r(v_{m})\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}\leqslant\omega(\|v_{m}\|)\,\|v_{m}\|.

Multiplying by
\sqrt{m}
yields

\sqrt{m}\,e_{\Omega_{m}}=\mathcal{L}(Z_{m})+\sqrt{m}\,r(v_{m}),\qquad\|\sqrt{m}\,r(v_{m})\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}\leqslant\omega(\|v_{m}\|)\,\|Z_{m}\|.

Lemma E.19

(The linearization remainder is negligible in probability)
.

Assume
B.5
and Assumption
E.4
. Then

\|\sqrt{m}\,r(v_{m})\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}\to 0\qquad\text{in probability.}

Proof.

Since
\Omega_{m}\to\Omega_{\star}
in probability and
v_{m}=\log_{\Omega_{\star}}(\Omega_{m})
on a normal neighborhood, we have
\|v_{m}\|\to 0

in probability. Moreover,
\|Z_{m}\|
is tight by the CLT.
Fix
\eta>0
and choose
t_{0}>0
such that
\omega(t)\leqslant\eta
for all
t\leqslant t_{0}
.
Then

\|\sqrt{m}\,r(v_{m})\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}\leqslant\eta\,\|Z_{m}\|\quad\text{on the event }\{\|v_{m}\|\leqslant t_{0}\}.

Since
\mathbb{P}(\|v_{m}\|\leqslant t_{0})\to 1
and
\|Z_{m}\|
is tight, the claim follows.
∎

Lemma E.20

(Quadratic approximation)
.

Assume
B.5
and Assumption
E.4
. Then

m\,\mathrm{Rep}(\Omega_{m})-\|\mathcal{L}(Z_{m})\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}^{2}\to 0\qquad\text{in probability.}

Proof.

Expanding the square,

m\,\mathrm{Rep}(\Omega_{m})=\|\sqrt{m}\,e_{\Omega_{m}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\|\mathcal{L}(Z_{m})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+2\langle\mathcal{L}(Z_{m}),\sqrt{m}\,r(v_{m})\rangle_{L^{2}}+\|\sqrt{m}\,r(v_{m})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

By (
E.6
),
\|\mathcal{L}(Z_{m})\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant C_{\mathcal{L}}\|Z_{m}\|
, hence
\|\mathcal{L}(Z_{m})\|_{L^{2}}
is tight.
By Lemma
E.19
,
\|\sqrt{m}\,r(v_{m})\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
in probability, which implies both the cross term and
the squared term vanish in probability.
∎

Proof of Proposition
E.17
.

By Lemma
E.20
, it suffices to identify the limit law of
\|\mathcal{L}(Z_{m})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
.
Since
Z_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}Z
in
T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}
and
z\mapsto\|\mathcal{L}(z)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
is continuous,
the continuous mapping theorem yields

\|\mathcal{L}(Z_{m})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\|\mathcal{L}(Z)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Combining with Lemma
E.20
gives the claim.
∎

Appendix F
Proofs of Section
6.1

This appendix verifies that the linear spectral contrastive model of

Section

6.1
satisfies the standing assumptions of Theorem
5.3
. Once these checks are in place, the corollary follows by a direct
invocation of Theorem
5.3
. We keep the verification modular and explicitly point to
the appendices where the general results are proved:
Appendix
E.1
controls the downstream estimation terms and provides the needed
perturbation/differentiability tools, while Appendix
E.2
provides the
1/m
pretraining
fluctuation analysis from the manifold CLT for
\hat{M}_{m}
.

F.1
Quotient feature map construction

Quotient feature map
\phi(x,M)
via a local section.

To express downstream prediction purely in quotient coordinates, we define a feature map that depends on the
descriptor
M\in\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
.
Fix a regular point
M_{\star}\in\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
and a neighborhood
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
of
M_{\star}

on which there exists a
C^{2}

local section

s:\mathcal{W}\to\mathbb{R}^{k\times d},\qquad s(M)^{\mathsf{T}}s(M)=M\ \ \text{for all }M\in\mathcal{W},

as constructed in Appendix
C.2
.
We then define the
quotient feature map

\displaystyle\phi(x,M)\coloneqq s(M)\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\qquad(x,M)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{W}.

(F.1)

Any two choices of local section differ by a left-multiplication
s^{\prime}(M)=Q(M)s(M)
with
Q(M)\in O(k)
,
hence they generate features related by an orthogonal transform.

Concrete local choice of the section
s(M)
.

Fix a reference point
M_{\star}\in\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
and choose an orthonormal basis

U_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
of
\mathrm{range}(M_{\star})
(so
U_{\star}^{\top}U_{\star}=I_{k}
).
For
M
in a sufficiently small neighborhood
\mathcal{W}
of
M_{\star}
, let
P(M)
denote the orthogonal projector
onto
\mathrm{range}(M)
. Define

\displaystyle B(M)\coloneqq U_{\star}^{\top}P(M)U_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k},\;U(M)\coloneqq P(M)U_{\star}\,B(M)^{-1/2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k},

so that
U(M)^{\top}U(M)=I_{k}
and
\mathrm{range}(U(M))=\mathrm{range}(M)
.
Next set

\Lambda(M)\coloneqq U(M)^{\top}MU(M)\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k},

which is positive definite on
\mathcal{W}
and satisfies
M=U(M)\Lambda(M)U(M)^{\top}
.
A concrete local section is then

s(M)\coloneqq\Lambda(M)^{1/2}U(M)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d},

so that
s(M)^{\top}s(M)=M
. Consequently, the quotient-level feature map can be written explicitly as

\phi(x,M)=s(M)x=\Lambda(M)^{1/2}U(M)^{\top}x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}.

All smoothness claims (existence of
\mathcal{W}
and differentiability of
M\mapsto s(M)
) are proved in
Appendix
C.2
.

F.2
Population descriptor problem and regularity of
M_{\star}

Population loss in descriptor space.

The following lemma expresses the population objective in terms of
M_{A}
.

Lemma F.1
.

Assume
\mathbb{E}\|x\|^{4}<\infty
.
Then

\displaystyle L_{\mathrm{spec}}(w)

\displaystyle=-2\,\Tr\!\big(M\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}\big)+\Tr\!\big((M\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})^{2}\big).

(F.2)

If furthermore
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}
is full-rank, then

\displaystyle L_{\mathrm{spec}}(w)

\displaystyle=\big\|\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{1/2}M\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{1/2}-C\big\|_{F}^{2}-\|C\|_{F}^{2},

(F.3)

where
C\coloneqq\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{-1/2}\,\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}\,\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{-1/2}
.

Proof.

Let
\Sigma\coloneqq\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}=\mathbb{E}[xx^{\top}]
. We have

\displaystyle L_{\mathrm{spec}}(w)

\displaystyle=-2\mathbb{E}[x^{\top}Mx^{+}]+\mathbb{E}[(x^{\top}Mx^{-})^{2}]=-2\tr(\mathbb{E}[Mx^{+}x^{\top})+\tr(\mathbb{E}[Mx(x^{-})^{\top}Mxx^{\top}])

\displaystyle=-2\tr(M\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+})+\tr(M\Sigma M\Sigma)

which proves (
F.2
).
If
\Sigma
is full rank, write

\Tr\!\big((M\Sigma)^{2}\big)=\|\Sigma^{1/2}M\Sigma^{1/2}\|_{F}^{2},\qquad\Tr(M\Sigma^{+})=\big\langle\Sigma^{1/2}M\Sigma^{1/2},\,C\big\rangle_{F},

with
C=\Sigma^{-1/2}\Sigma^{+}\Sigma^{-1/2}
.
Completing the square gives (
F.3
).
∎

Remark F.2
.

Even though
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}
and
M
are PSD, the matrix
C
can be indefinite:
augmentations may induce negative correlations along some directions.

Uniqueness of the descriptor minimizer.

By
Lemma

F.1
, the population minimization in descriptor space reduces to

\displaystyle M_{\star}\in\argmin_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{d,k}}\ \big\|\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{1/2}M\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{1/2}-C\big\|_{F}^{2}.

(F.4)

We now state a simple eigengap condition that guarantees uniqueness.

Assume throughout
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}\succ 0
and
6.1
. Recall the whitened matrix

C\coloneqq\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{-1/2},

and the descriptor manifold
\mathcal{M}_{d,k}\coloneqq\{M\succcurlyeq 0:\rank(M)=k\}
.
Thus, the population minimizer
M_{\star}
exists, is unique,
and satisfies

\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{1/2}M_{\star}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{1/2}=U_{k}\Lambda_{k}U_{k}^{\top},

where
U_{k}\Lambda_{k}U_{k}^{\top}
is the rank-
k
truncation onto the top-
k

positive
eigenvalues of
C
.
In particular,
M_{\star}\in\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
is a regular point of the fixed-rank PSD manifold.

Lemma F.3

(Local rank stability for
M_{\star}
(
E.1
))
.

Under
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}\succ 0
and
6.1
, there exists a neighborhood

\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
of
M_{\star}
and constants
\kappa_{\star},K_{\star}\in(0,\infty)
such that,
for all
M\in\mathcal{U}
,

\rank(M)=k,\qquad\lambda_{k}(M)\geqslant\kappa_{\star},\qquad\|M\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant K_{\star}.

Proof.

Since
M_{\star}\in\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
has
k
strictly positive eigenvalues, we have
\lambda_{k}(M_{\star})>0
.
Eigenvalues depend continuously on
M
in operator norm by Weyl’s inequality.
Hence there exists an operator-norm neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
