M_{\star}
such that

\lambda_{k}(M)\geqslant\lambda_{k}(M_{\star})/2
for all
M\in\mathcal{U}
, which implies
\rank(M)=k
on
\mathcal{U}
.
The operator-norm bound follows similarly by continuity of
M\mapsto\|M\|_{\mathrm{op}}
.
∎

Lemma
F.3
is the model-specific input needed to place the analysis on the
regular stratum, which is exactly the regime required in Appendix
E.1
and
Appendix
E.2
for differentiability of projector-valued maps and stable-rank pseudoinverse calculus.

F.3
Quotient features and smooth dependence on
M

Fix the regular point
M_{\star}\in\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
and let
s(\cdot)
be the
C^{2}
local section from
Appendix
C.2
(equivalently, the concrete construction in
Section

6.1
),
defined on a neighborhood
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
of
M_{\star}
and satisfying
s(M)^{\top}s(M)=M
.
Define the quotient feature map

\phi(x,M)\coloneqq s(M)x\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\qquad(x,M)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{W}.

By Appendix
C.2
, the map
M\mapsto s(M)
is
C^{2}
on
\mathcal{W}
, hence

M\mapsto\phi(x,M)
is
C^{1}
for each fixed
x
.

Lemma F.4

(Local-uniform feature and derivative moments(
E.2
and
E.4
))
.

Assume
\mathbb{E}\|X\|^{4+\delta}<\infty
for some
\delta>0
, where
X\sim\mu_{\mathrm{down}}
.
Then there exists a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{W}
of
M_{\star}
and a constant
C<\infty
such that

\sup_{M\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{E}\|\phi(X,M)\|^{4+\delta}\leqslant C,\qquad\sup_{M\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{E}\|D_{M}\phi(X,M)\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{4+\delta}\leqslant C.

Proof.

Since
s(\cdot)
is
C^{2}
on
\mathcal{W}
, its operator norm and the operator norm of its derivative are locally bounded.
Choose a relatively compact neighborhood
\mathcal{U}\Subset\mathcal{W}
of
M_{\star}
so that

\sup_{M\in\mathcal{U}}\|s(M)\|_{\mathrm{op}}<\infty,\qquad\sup_{M\in\mathcal{U}}\|D_{M}s(M)\|_{\mathrm{op}}<\infty.

Then
\|\phi(X,M)\|\leqslant\|s(M)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|X\|
and

\|D_{M}\phi(X,M)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\|D_{M}s(M)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|X\|
.
Raising to the power
4+\delta
and taking expectations yields the stated bounds.
∎

Lemma
F.4
matches the local-uniform moment assumptions used in
Appendix
E.1
(and in the projector differentiability result stated there).

F.4
Downstream covariance, stable rank, and effective dimension

Write
\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[XX^{\top}]
and assume
\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}\succ 0
.
For
M\in\mathcal{U}
, define the downstream feature covariance

\Sigma(M)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[\phi(X,M)\phi(X,M)^{\top}]=\mathbb{E}[s(M)XX^{\top}s(M)^{\top}]=s(M)\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}s(M)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}.

Lemma F.5

(Stable rank/eigengap for
\Sigma(M)
near
M_{\star}
)
.

Assume
\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}\succ 0
and let
\mathcal{U}
be as in Lemma
F.4
.
Then there exist constants
\kappa_{\Sigma},K_{\Sigma}\in(0,\infty)
such that, for all
M\in\mathcal{U}
,

\rank(\Sigma(M))=k,\qquad\lambda_{k}(\Sigma(M))\geqslant\kappa_{\Sigma},\qquad\|\Sigma(M)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant K_{\Sigma}.

In particular,
d_{\mathrm{eff}}(M)=\tr(\Sigma(M)\Sigma(M)^{+})=k
for all
M\in\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

For each
M\in\mathcal{U}
, the matrix
s(M)\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
has rank
k
because
s(M)^{\top}s(M)=M
and
\rank(M)=k
.
Since
\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}\succ 0
, the product
s(M)\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}s(M)^{\top}
is positive definite on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
hence
\rank(\Sigma(M))=k
.
The eigenvalue bounds follow from continuity of
M\mapsto s(M)
and compactness of
\mathcal{U}
:

\lambda_{k}(\Sigma(M))=\lambda_{\min}(s(M)\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}s(M)^{\top})

is continuous in
M
and strictly positive for each
M
, so it has a positive minimum on
\mathcal{U}
.
Similarly,
\|\Sigma(M)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
attains a finite maximum on
\mathcal{U}
.
Finally, for invertible
\Sigma(M)
one has
\Sigma(M)\Sigma(M)^{+}=I_{k}
, hence
d_{\mathrm{eff}}(M)=k
.
∎

Lemma
F.5
verifies the stable rank/eigengap condition for the downstream covariance
used throughout Appendix
E.1
.

F.5
Well-posedness of the empirical projector

Recall that
\mathcal{H}_{M}=\{x\mapsto\theta^{\top}\phi(x,M):\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\}
is a
k
-dimensional linear class.
The well-posedness assumption of Appendix
E.1
is equivalent to requiring that the
empirical inner product is non-degenerate on
\mathcal{H}_{M}
, or equivalently that the
k\times k
empirical
covariance
\Sigma_{n}(M)
has full rank.

Lemma F.6

(Well-posedness holds almost surely for nondegenerate designs(
E.3
))
.

Assume
\mu_{\mathrm{down}}
is nondegenerate in the sense that
X
has a density on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}\succ 0
. Fix any
M\in\mathcal{U}
and any
n\geqslant k
.
Then, with probability one over
X_{1:n}
,

\rank\!\Big(\Sigma_{n}(M)\Big)=k,\qquad\Sigma_{n}(M)\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi(X_{i},M)\phi(X_{i},M)^{\top}.

Consequently, the empirical projector
\Pi_{M,n}
acts as the identity on
\mathcal{H}_{M}
.

Proof.

Write
\Phi\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
for the design matrix with rows
\phi(X_{i},M)^{\top}
.
Then
\Sigma_{n}(M)=\frac{1}{n}\Phi^{\top}\Phi
has rank
k
if and only if
\Phi
has rank
k
.
Since
\phi(X_{i},M)=s(M)X_{i}
and
s(M)
has rank
k
, the random vector
\phi(X_{i},M)
has a density on
\mathbb{R}^{k}

(because
X_{i}
has a density on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
s(M)
is a surjective linear map
\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
).
For i.i.d. vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
with a density, the event that
k
of them fall into a common proper hyperplane has probability
0
,
so
\Phi
has rank
k
almost surely when
n\geqslant k
.
The final claim is exactly the well-posedness implication used in Appendix
E.1
.
∎

Since
M_{m}
is independent of the downstream sample, Lemma
F.6
applies conditionally on
M_{m}

and yields the well-posedness requirement along the triangular array
(M_{m},n)
used in the master theorem.

Next, we show that the moment condition
\mathbb{E}[\|X\|^{4}]<\infty
is in fact sufficient for
E.3
.

Proposition F.7

(
26
)
.

Fix a
\delta\in(0,1)
and suppose
\lVert x\rVert_{L^{4}}<\infty
.
Define the constants:

\displaystyle C_{X}\coloneq\sup_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[\langle(\Sigma^{+})^{1/2}x,v\rangle^{4}]},\quad k\coloneq\rank(\Sigma).

(F.5)

Suppose that
n\geqslant c_{0}C_{X}^{2}(k+\log(1/\delta))
for a universal
c_{0}
. Then with probability at least
1-\delta
:

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n}\succcurlyeq\frac{1}{4}\Sigma.

On this event, we also have that
\mathrm{Col}(\Sigma_{n})=\mathrm{Col}(\Sigma)
.

Proof.

The first part of the claim, that
\Sigma_{n}\succcurlyeq\frac{1}{4}\Sigma
, is immediate from
26
.
To finish, suppose that
\Sigma_{n}\succcurlyeq\frac{1}{4}\Sigma
holds.
Now, let
q\in\mathrm{Kern}(\Sigma_{n})
. By the above, this implies that

\displaystyle 0=q^{\mathsf{T}}\Sigma_{n}q\geqslant\frac{1}{4}q^{\mathsf{T}}\Sigma q\geqslant 0,

and hence
q^{\mathsf{T}}\Sigma q=0
, which implies
q\in\mathrm{Kern}(\Sigma^{1/2})=\mathrm{Kern}(\Sigma)
.
Therefore,
\mathrm{Kern}(\Sigma_{n})\subseteq\mathrm{Kern}(\Sigma)
, which by
Lemma

E.9
implies

\mathrm{Col}(\Sigma_{n})=\mathrm{Col}(\Sigma)
.
∎

F.6
Differentiability of the population projector map
M\mapsto\Pi_{M}

For
M\in\mathcal{U}
, define
T_{M}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
by
(T_{M}\theta)(x)=\theta^{\top}\phi(x,M)
.
The population projector onto
\mathcal{H}_{M}=\mathrm{Im}(T_{M})
admits the closed form

\Pi_{M}g=T_{M}\,\Sigma(M)^{+}\,c_{g}(M),\qquad c_{g}(M)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[\phi(X,M)g(X)].

In Appendix
E.1
, Proposition
E.6
proves Fréchet differentiability of

M\mapsto\Pi_{M}
at
M_{\star}
under:
(i) stable rank/eigengap (Lemma
F.5
),
(ii) local-uniform moments (Lemma
F.4
),
and (iii)
C^{1}
dependence of
\phi(\cdot,M)
with a suitable derivative moment bound
(Lemma
F.4
).
Therefore the differentiability assumption used in Appendix
E.2
is satisfied in this model.

F.7
Pretraining consistency and manifold CLT for the linear spectral loss

In this subsection we derive the asymptotic normality of the descriptor estimator
\hat{M}_{m}
directly from
the structure of the linear spectral loss and the pretraining data model, without appealing to the abstract

M
-estimation appendix beyond standard empirical-process inputs (LLN and CLT for i.i.d. averages).

Model and loss.

A pretraining observation is
z=(x,x^{+},x^{-})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{3}
, where
(x,x^{+})
is a positive pair and
x^{-}
is an
independent negative:
x^{-}
is an independent copy of
x
, independent of
(x,x^{+})
.
Assume
\mathbb{E}[x]=0
and define

\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[xx^{\top}],\qquad\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[x^{+}(x)^{\top}].

For
M\in\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
, recall the per-sample loss (well-defined for all symmetric
M
)

\ell_{\mathrm{spec}}(M;z)=-2\,x^{\top}Mx^{+}+(x^{\top}Mx^{-})^{2}.

We minimize the empirical loss over the rank-
k
PSD manifold
\mathcal{M}_{d,k}=\{M\succcurlyeq 0:\rank(M)=k\}
:

\hat{L}_{m}(M)\coloneqq\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ell_{\mathrm{spec}}(M;z_{j}),\qquad\hat{M}_{m}\in\argmin_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{d,k}}\hat{L}_{m}(M).

Let
L(M)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[\ell_{\mathrm{spec}}(M;z)]
be the population loss.

Moment assumption (for LLN and CLT of derivatives).

Assume there exists
\delta>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\|x\|^{8+\delta}<\infty,\qquad\mathbb{E}\big[\|x\|^{4+\delta}\|x^{+}\|^{4+\delta}\big]<\infty.

(F.6)

This ensures integrability of the score and Hessian random fields used below.

F.7.1
Population objective, score, and Hessian

Let
\operatorname{sym}(A)\coloneqq(A+A^{\top})/2
. Viewing
\ell_{\mathrm{spec}}(\cdot;z)
as a function on
\mathcal{M}_{d,k}

with the Frobenius inner product, its Euclidean gradient is

\nabla_{M}\ell_{\mathrm{spec}}(M;z)=-2\,\operatorname{sym}(x(x^{+})^{\top})+2\,(x^{\top}Mx^{-})\,\operatorname{sym}(x(x^{-})^{\top}).

(F.7)

Its Euclidean Hessian is the linear map
H\mapsto D(\nabla_{M}\ell_{\mathrm{spec}})(M;z)[H]
given by

D(\nabla_{M}\ell_{\mathrm{spec}})(M;z)[H]=2\,(x^{\top}Hx^{-})\,\operatorname{sym}(x(x^{-})^{\top}).

(F.8)

Taking expectations and using
x^{-}
independent of
(x,x^{+})
with
\mathbb{E}[x^{-}(x^{-})^{\top}]=\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla L(M)

\displaystyle=-2\,\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}+2\,\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}\,M\,\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}},

(F.9)

\displaystyle D(\nabla L)(M)[H]

\displaystyle=2\,\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}\,H\,\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}.

(F.10)

In particular, the population Hessian is constant (independent of
M
) and is positive definite on
\mathcal{M}_{d,k`}
since

\langle H,\,2\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}\rangle_{F}=2\|\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{1/2}H\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{1/2}\|_{F}^{2}.

F.7.2
Consistency of
\hat{M}_{m}

We record a standard consistency statement.

Lemma F.8

(Consistency)
.

Assume
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}\succ 0
,
6.1
, and (
F.6
). Then

\hat{M}_{m}\to M_{\star}\qquad\text{in probability.}

Proof sketch.

By (
F.9
)–(
F.10
) and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}\succ 0
, the population objective is coercive on

\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
:
L(M)\to\infty
as
\|M\|_{F}\to\infty
within
\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
.
Hence the sublevel set
\{M\in\mathcal{M}_{d,k}:L(M)\leqslant L(M_{\star})+1\}
is compact (in the induced topology on
\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
).
Under (
F.6
),
\ell_{\mathrm{spec}}(M;z)
is integrable uniformly over that compact set and

\hat{L}_{m}\to L
uniformly in probability on it (standard uniform LLN for finite-dimensional parametric charts on compact sets).
Since
M_{\star}
is the unique minimizer, the argmin theorem yields

\hat{M}_{m}\to M_{\star}
in probability.
∎

F.7.3
Manifold CLT in normal coordinates

Let
\log_{M_{\star}}(\cdot)
be the Riemannian log map on the smooth manifold
\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
(with the induced metric)
defined on a normal neighborhood of
M_{\star}
, and define the tangent error

v_{m}\coloneqq\log_{M_{\star}}(\hat{M}_{m})\in T_{M_{\star}}\mathcal{M}_{d,k}.

Write
\operatorname{Proj}_{T}
for the orthogonal projection (in Frobenius) onto
T_{M_{\star}}\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
.
Define the tangent-space score random vector (a tangent element)

\varphi(z)\coloneqq\operatorname{Proj}_{T}\big(\nabla_{M}\ell_{\mathrm{spec}}(M_{\star};z)\big)\in T_{M_{\star}}\mathcal{M}_{d,k},\qquad\Sigma_{\star}\coloneqq\operatorname{Cov}(\varphi(z)).

(F.11)

Define the (Riemannian) Hessian operator on the tangent space

H_{\star}\coloneqq\operatorname{Hess}L(M_{\star}):T_{M_{\star}}\mathcal{M}_{d,k}\to T_{M_{\star}}\mathcal{M}_{d,k}.

(F.12)

Lemma F.9

(Nondegeneracy of the tangent Hessian)
.

Assume
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}\succ 0
and that
M_{\star}
is a (Riemannian) local minimizer of
L
on
\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
.
Then
H_{\star}
is positive definite and hence invertible. Moreover, with the induced Frobenius metric,

\langle v,H_{\star}v\rangle=2\|\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{1/2}v\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{1/2}\|_{F}^{2}\qquad\text{for all }v\in T_{M_{\star}}\mathcal{M}_{d,k}.

Proof.

Work in normal coordinates
M=\exp_{M_{\star}}(v)
on a neighborhood of
M_{\star}
.
The ambient Taylor expansion of
L
on
\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
at
M_{\star}
is exact to second order since
(
F.10
) is constant:

L(M_{\star}+\Delta)=L(M_{\star})+\langle\nabla L(M_{\star}),\Delta\rangle_{F}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\Delta,\,2\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}\Delta\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}\rangle_{F}.

For
M=\exp_{M_{\star}}(v)
, we have
M=M_{\star}+v+o(\|v\|)
in the ambient space.
Since
M_{\star}
is a Riemannian minimizer on
\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
, the Riemannian gradient vanishes, which means

\operatorname{Proj}_{T}(\nabla L(M_{\star}))=0
. Equivalently,
\nabla L(M_{\star})
is orthogonal to
T_{M_{\star}}\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
,
so
\langle\nabla L(M_{\star}),v\rangle_{F}=0
for all
v\in T_{M_{\star}}\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
.
Plugging
\Delta=v+o(\|v\|)
into the expansion yields

L(\exp_{M_{\star}}(v))=L(M_{\star})+\frac{1}{2}\langle v,\,2\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}v\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}\rangle_{F}+o(\|v\|^{2}),

which identifies the Riemannian Hessian quadratic form at
M_{\star}
on tangent directions as claimed.
Since
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}\succ 0
, the form is strictly positive for
v\neq 0
, hence
H_{\star}
is invertible.
∎

Proposition F.10

(CLT for
\hat{M}_{m}
in normal coordinates)
.

Assume
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}\succ 0
,
6.1
, and (
F.6
).
Let
\hat{M}_{m}
be a measurable empirical minimizer of
\hat{L}_{m}
over
\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
.
Then

\sqrt{m}\,\log_{M_{\star}}(\hat{M}_{m})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}Z,\qquad Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,V),\quad V\coloneqq H_{\star}^{-1}\Sigma_{\star}H_{\star}^{-1},

as a random element in
T_{M_{\star}}\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
.

Proof.

Let
v_{m}\coloneqq\log_{M_{\star}}(\hat{M}_{m})
. By Lemma
F.8
,
v_{m}\to 0
in probability and,
for all large
m
,
\hat{M}_{m}
lies in a normal neighborhood where the log map is defined.

Define the empirical Riemannian gradient at
M\in\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
by

\operatorname{grad}\hat{L}_{m}(M)\coloneqq\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\operatorname{Proj}_{T_{M}}\big(\nabla_{M}\ell_{\mathrm{spec}}(M;z_{j})\big),

and similarly
\operatorname{grad}L(M)
.
Since
\hat{M}_{m}
is a minimizer,
\operatorname{grad}\hat{L}_{m}(\hat{M}_{m})=0
.
Transport gradients to the fixed tangent space
T_{M_{\star}}\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
using normal coordinates and apply a Taylor expansion:

0=\operatorname{grad}\hat{L}_{m}(\exp_{M_{\star}}(v_{m}))=\operatorname{grad}\hat{L}_{m}(M_{\star})+H_{\star}v_{m}+r_{m},

where
r_{m}=o_{p}(\|v_{m}\|)+o_{p}(m^{-1/2})
. This remainder control follows from the smoothness of

M\mapsto\operatorname{Proj}_{T_{M}}
on the regular stratum and the moment bound (
F.6
),
which implies local uniform integrability of the first two derivatives of
\ell_{\mathrm{spec}}(M;z)
.

Next, by definition of
\varphi(z)
in (
F.11
),

\sqrt{m}\,\operatorname{grad}\hat{L}_{m}(M_{\star})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\varphi(z_{j}),\qquad\mathbb{E}[\varphi(z)]=0,

and (
F.6
) implies
\mathbb{E}\|\varphi(z)\|^{2}<\infty
. Hence the multivariate CLT in the finite-dimensional
space
T_{M_{\star}}\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
yields

\sqrt{m}\,\operatorname{grad}\hat{L}_{m}(M_{\star})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\star}).

Lemma
F.9
gives invertibility of
H_{\star}
. Solving the linearized equation gives

\sqrt{m}\,v_{m}=-\,H_{\star}^{-1}\sqrt{m}\,\operatorname{grad}\hat{L}_{m}(M_{\star})+o_{p}(1),

and Slutsky’s theorem yields
\sqrt{m}\,v_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,H_{\star}^{-1}\Sigma_{\star}H_{\star}^{-1})
.
∎

F.8
Explicit Calculations of the Concrete Example

In this section, we first derive an asymptotic distribution for the spectral pretraining estimator in a general Gaussian model by computing the closed-form characterization of the limiting covariance operator
V_{\star}=H_{\star}^{-1}\Sigma_{\star}H_{\star}^{-1}
in Proposition
F.10
. We then specialize the result to the concrete diagonal example
presented in
Section

6.1
.

Fully Gaussian assumption.

Assume
(X,X^{+})
is jointly Gaussian, and
X^{-}
is an independent copy of
X
,
independent of
(X,X^{+})
. In particular,

\mathbb{E}[XX^{\top}]=\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}},\qquad\mathbb{E}[X(X^{+})^{\top}]=\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}.

Bilinear form for the score covariance.

Let
\mathcal{S}^{k}
be the space of
d
by
d
symmetric matrices. For a symmetric direction
H\in\mathcal{S}^{d}
, define the scalar score functional at
M_{\star}
by

S_{H}(Z)\coloneqq\langle\nabla_{M}\ell_{\mathrm{spec}}(M_{\star};Z),H\rangle=-2\,X^{\top}HX^{+}+2\,(X^{\top}M_{\star}X^{-})\,(X^{\top}HX^{-}).

where
Z=(X,X^{+},X^{-})
. Recall that
\Sigma_{\star}=\operatorname{Cov}(\varphi(Z))
with
\varphi(Z)=\operatorname{Proj}_{T}(\nabla_{M}\ell_{\mathrm{spec}}(M_{\star};Z))
where
\operatorname{Proj}_{T}
is the orthogonal projection onto
T_{M_{\star}}\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
. We know that
\operatorname{Proj}_{T}
is self-adjoint and
\mathbb{E}[\varphi(Z)]=0
. Thus, for all
v_{1},v_{2}\in T_{M_{\star}}\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
we have

\langle v_{1},\Sigma_{\star}v_{2}\rangle=\operatorname{Cov}\!\big(S_{v_{1}}(Z),S_{v_{2}}(Z)\big).

(F.13)

The following proposition gives
\operatorname{Cov}(S_{H_{1}},S_{H_{2}})
in closed form under joint Gaussianity.

Proposition F.11

(Exact score covariance under joint Gaussianity)
.

Under the fully Gaussian assumption, for any
H_{1},H_{2}\in\mathcal{S}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}\!\big(S_{H_{1}}(Z),S_{H_{2}}(Z)\big)=4\Big(C_{aa}(H_{1},H_{2})-C_{ab}(H_{1},H_{2})-C_{ab}(H_{2},H_{1})+C_{bb}(H_{1},H_{2})\Big),

(F.14)

where the terms are given as follows:

(i)

Positive-pair term:

\displaystyle C_{aa}(H_{1},H_{2})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tr(H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})+\tr(H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}H_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}).

(F.15)

(ii)

Cross term: Let
P_{i}\coloneqq M_{\star}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{i}
. Then

\displaystyle C_{ab}(H_{1},H_{2})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Tr(P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+})+\Tr(P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}).

(F.16)

(iii)

Negative-sample term:
Let
Q\coloneqq M_{\star}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}M_{\star}
.
Then

\displaystyle C_{bb}(H_{1},H_{2})

\displaystyle=\tr(P_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})\tr(P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})+2\tr(P_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})+\tr(H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})\tr(Q\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})

\displaystyle\quad+4\Tr(H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}Q\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}).

(F.17)

We will repeatedly use the following standard lemmas in the proof.

Lemma F.12

(Bilinear–bilinear moment)
.

Let
(U,V)
be jointly Gaussian with
\mathbb{E}[U]=\mathbb{E}[V]=0
,

\mathbb{E}[UU^{\top}]=\Sigma_{U}
,
\mathbb{E}[VV^{\top}]=\Sigma_{V}
, and
\mathbb{E}[UV^{\top}]=\Sigma_{UV}
.
Then for any
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[(U^{\top}AV)(U^{\top}BV)\big]

\displaystyle=\Tr(A\Sigma_{UV})\Tr(B\Sigma_{UV})+\Tr(A\Sigma_{U}B\Sigma_{V})+\Tr(A\Sigma_{UV}B^{\top}\Sigma_{UV}^{\top}).

(F.18)

Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}(U^{\top}AV,U^{\top}BV)

\displaystyle=\Tr(A\Sigma_{U}B\Sigma_{V})+\Tr(A\Sigma_{UV}B^{\top}\Sigma_{UV}^{\top}).

(F.19)

Lemma F.13

(Quadratic–quadratic moment)
.

Let
U\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
and let
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[(U^{\top}AU)(U^{\top}BU)\big]

\displaystyle=\Tr(A\Sigma)\Tr(B\Sigma)+\Tr(A\Sigma B\Sigma)+\Tr(A\Sigma B^{\top}\Sigma).

(F.20)

Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}(U^{\top}AU,U^{\top}BU)

\displaystyle=\Tr(A\Sigma B\Sigma)+\Tr(A\Sigma B^{\top}\Sigma).

(F.21)

Proof of Lemmas
F.12
–
F.13
.

Both identities follow by expanding the products componentwise and applying Isserlis’ formula to fourth moments.
For instance, for Lemma
F.12
,

\mathbb{E}[(U^{\top}AV)(U^{\top}BV)]=\sum_{i,j,p,q}A_{ij}B_{pq}\,\mathbb{E}[U_{i}V_{j}U_{p}V_{q}],

and Isserlis gives

\mathbb{E}[U_{i}V_{j}U_{p}V_{q}]=\mathbb{E}[U_{i}V_{j}]\mathbb{E}[U_{p}V_{q}]+\mathbb{E}[U_{i}U_{p}]\mathbb{E}[V_{j}V_{q}]+\mathbb{E}[U_{i}V_{q}]\mathbb{E}[V_{j}U_{p}]
,
which sums to (
F.18
). Lemma
F.13
is analogous.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

F.11
.

Write
S_{H}(Z)=-2a_{H}(Z)+2b_{H}(Z)
with

a_{H}(Z)\coloneqq X^{\top}HX^{+},\qquad b_{H}(Z)\coloneqq(X^{\top}M_{\star}X^{-})(X^{\top}HX^{-}).

Then, for any
H_{1},H_{2}\in\mathcal{S}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}(S_{H_{1}}(Z),S_{H_{2}}(Z))

\displaystyle=4\Big(\mathrm{Cov}(a_{H_{1}},a_{H_{2}})-\mathrm{Cov}(a_{H_{1}},b_{H_{2}})-\mathrm{Cov}(a_{H_{2}},b_{H_{1}})+\mathrm{Cov}(b_{H_{1}},b_{H_{2}})\Big).

(F.22)

We compute the four covariance terms under joint Gaussianity using Isserlis’ formula.

First, we will compute
\mathrm{Cov}(a_{H_{1}},a_{H_{2}})
. Apply Lemma
F.12
with
(U,V)=(X,X^{+})
,

\Sigma_{U}=\Sigma_{V}=\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}
and
\Sigma_{UV}=\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}
.
Since
H_{1},H_{2}
are symmetric and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}
is symmetric,
(
F.19
) yields

C_{aa}(H_{1},H_{2})=\mathrm{Cov}(a_{H_{1}},a_{H_{2}})=\Tr(H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})+\Tr(H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}H_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}).

Next, we will compute the cross term
\mathrm{Cov}(a_{H_{1}},b_{H_{2}})
. Fix
H_{1},H_{2}
and write

b_{H_{2}}=(X^{\top}M_{\star}X^{-})(X^{\top}H_{2}X^{-})=(X^{-})^{\top}(M_{\star}XX^{\top}H_{2})X^{-}.

Condition on
(X,X^{+})
and use that
X^{-}
is independent of
(X,X^{+})
with covariance
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[b_{H_{2}}\mid X,X^{+}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big[(X^{\top}M_{\star}X^{-})(X^{\top}H_{2}X^{-})\mid X,X^{+}\big]=X^{\top}M_{\star}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{2}X.

(F.23)

Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[a_{H_{1}}b_{H_{2}}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[(X^{\top}H_{1}X^{+})\,\mathbb{E}[b_{H_{2}}\mid X,X^{+}]\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[(X^{\top}H_{1}X^{+})\,(X^{\top}P_{2}X)\Big],

(F.24)

where
B_{2}\coloneqq M_{\star}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{2}
.

We next compute the mixed moment in (
F.24
). Expand

(X^{\top}H_{1}X^{+})(X^{\top}P_{2}X)=\sum_{i,j,p,q}(H_{1})_{ij}(P_{2})_{pq}\,X_{i}X^{+}_{j}X_{p}X_{q}.

Apply Isserlis to
\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X^{+}_{j}X_{p}X_{q}]
for the jointly Gaussian pair
(X,X^{+})
:

\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X^{+}_{j}X_{p}X_{q}]=\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X^{+}_{j}]\mathbb{E}[X_{p}X_{q}]+\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X_{p}]\mathbb{E}[X^{+}_{j}X_{q}]+\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X_{q}]\mathbb{E}[X^{+}_{j}X_{p}].

Using
\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X^{+}_{j}]=(\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+})_{ij}
and
\mathbb{E}[X_{p}X_{q}]=(\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})_{pq}
,
this yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[(X^{\top}H_{1}X^{+})(X^{\top}P_{2}X)\big]

\displaystyle=\Tr(H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+})\Tr(P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})+\Tr(P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+})+\Tr(P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}).

(F.25)

Moreover,

\mathbb{E}[a_{H_{1}}]=\Tr(H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}),\qquad\mathbb{E}[b_{H_{2}}]=\mathbb{E}[X^{\top}P_{2}X]=\Tr(P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}).

Therefore, subtracting
\mathbb{E}[a_{H_{1}}]\mathbb{E}[b_{H_{2}}]
from (
F.25
) gives

C_{ab}(H_{1},H_{2})=\mathrm{Cov}(a_{H_{1}},b_{H_{2}})=\Tr(P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+})+\Tr(P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}).

Finally, we will compute the negative-sample term
\mathrm{Cov}(b_{H_{1}},b_{H_{2}})
. Write

b_{H_{i}}=(X^{\top}M_{\star}X^{-})(X^{\top}H_{i}X^{-})=(X^{-})^{\top}A_{i}(X)X^{-},\qquad A_{i}(X)\coloneqq M_{\star}XX^{\top}H_{i}.

From the total law of covariance, we have

C_{bb}(H_{1},H_{2})=\mathrm{Cov}(b_{H_{1}},b_{H_{2}})=\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Cov}(b_{H_{1}},b_{H_{2}}|X)]+\mathrm{Cov}(\mathbb{E}[b_{H_{1}}|X],\mathbb{E}[b_{H_{2}}|X]).

We apply Lemma
F.13
to
X^{-}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}(b_{H_{1}},b_{H_{2}}\mid X)

\displaystyle=\Tr(A_{1}(X)\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}A_{2}(X)\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})+\Tr(A_{1}(X)\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}A_{2}(X)^{T}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})

\displaystyle=(X^{\mathsf{T}}M_{\star}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{1}XX^{\mathsf{T}}M_{\star}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{2}X)+(X^{\mathsf{T}}H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{2}XX^{\mathsf{T}}M_{\star}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}M_{\star}X)

\displaystyle=(X^{\mathsf{T}}P_{1}XX^{\mathsf{T}}P_{2}X)+(X^{\mathsf{T}}H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{2}XX^{\mathsf{T}}QX).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Cov}(b_{H_{1}},b_{H_{2}}|X)]

\displaystyle=\tr(P_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})\tr(P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})+\tr(P_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})+\tr(H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})\tr(Q\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})

\displaystyle\quad+3\Tr(H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}Q\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}).

Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}(\mathbb{E}[b_{H_{1}}|X],\mathbb{E}[b_{H_{2}}|X])=\mathrm{Cov}(X^{\mathsf{T}}P_{1}X,X^{\mathsf{T}}P_{2}X)=\tr(P_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})+\tr(H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}Q\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}).

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle C_{bb}(H_{1},H_{2})

\displaystyle=\tr(P_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})\tr(P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})+2\tr(P_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})+\tr(H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})\tr(Q\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})

\displaystyle\quad+4\Tr(H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}Q\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}).

∎

Corollary F.14
.

Under the fully Gaussian assumption, for any
H_{1},H_{2}\in\mathcal{S}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle H_{1},V_{\star}H_{2}\rangle=C_{aa}(H^{\prime}_{1},H^{\prime}_{2})-C_{ab}(H^{\prime}_{1},H^{\prime}_{2})-C_{ab}(H^{\prime}_{2},H^{\prime}_{1})+C_{bb}(H^{\prime}_{1},H^{\prime}_{2})

(F.26)

where
H_{i}^{\prime}=\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{-1}H_{i}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{-1}
for
i=\{1,2\}
.

Proof.

According to
Lemma

F.9
, for any
v\in\mathcal{S}^{d}
, we have

H_{\star}v=2\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}v\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}},\qquad H_{\star}^{-1}v=\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{-1}v\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{-1}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\langle H_{1},V_{\star}H_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle H_{1},H_{\star}^{-1}\Sigma_{\star}H_{\star}^{-1}H_{2}\rangle=\langle H_{\star}^{-1}H_{1},\Sigma_{\star}H_{\star}^{-1}H_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\langle\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{-1}H_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{-1},\Sigma_{\star}H_{\star}^{-1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{-1}H_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{-1}\rangle=\frac{1}{4}\langle H_{1}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\star}H_{2}^{\prime}\rangle=\frac{1}{4}\operatorname{Cov}\!\big(S_{H^{\prime}_{1}}(Z),S_{H^{\prime}_{2}}(Z)\big).

Equation

F.26
follows after plugging this equation into
Equation

F.14
.
∎

Linearization of the representation error.

Fix
M
in a neighborhood of
M_{\star}
and let
A:=s(M)\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
be the local
section so that
A^{\top}A=M
. Consider the operator

T_{M}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
defined by

(T_{M}b)(x):=\langle b,Ax\rangle=b^{\top}Ax,\qquad b\in\mathbb{R}^{k}.

Its adjoint
T_{M}^{\mathrm{adj}}:L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
satisfies, for any

g\in L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
,

T_{M}^{\mathrm{adj}}g=\mathbb{E}\big[g(X)\,AX\big]=A\,\mathbb{E}\big[g(X)\,X\big],

where
X\sim\mu_{\mathrm{down}}
and we used linearity of
A
.

Let

\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}:=\mathbb{E}[XX^{\top}],\qquad\Sigma(M):=T_{M}^{\mathrm{adj}}T_{M}=\mathbb{E}[(AX)(AX)^{\top}]=A\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}A^{\top}.

The orthogonal projector onto
\mathrm{Range}(T_{M})
is given by

\Pi_{M}=T_{M}\,\Sigma(M)^{+}\,T_{M}^{\mathrm{adj}}.

Therefore, for any
g\in L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
,

\displaystyle(\Pi_{M}g)(x)

\displaystyle=\big(T_{M}\Sigma(M)^{+}T_{M}^{\mathrm{adj}}g\big)(x)=\left\langle\Sigma(M)^{+}\,T_{M}^{\mathrm{adj}}g,\ Ax\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\Sigma(M)^{+}\,A\,\mathbb{E}[g(X)X],\ Ax\right\rangle=x^{\top}A^{\top}\Sigma(M)^{+}A\,\mathbb{E}[g(X)X].

(F.27)

Define the induced matrix

P(M):=A^{\top}\Sigma(M)^{+}A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}.

Then (
F.27
) becomes the compact form

(\Pi_{M}g)(x)=x^{\top}P(M)\,\mathbb{E}[g(X)X].

We know that the target function is
f_{\star}(x)=\beta_{\star}^{\top}A_{\star}x
for some
\beta_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}

and
A_{\star}:=s(M_{\star})
. Then

\mathbb{E}[f_{\star}(X)X]=\mathbb{E}[XX^{\top}]A_{\star}^{\top}\beta_{\star}=\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}A_{\star}^{\top}\beta_{\star},

and therefore

(\Pi_{M}f_{\star})(x)=x^{\top}P(M)\,\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}\,A_{\star}^{\top}\beta_{\star}.

(F.28)

We linearize (
F.28
) around
M_{\star}
using

Proposition

E.6
. In the constant-rank region, the map

\Omega\mapsto\Pi_{\Omega}
is Fréchet differentiable at
\Omega_{\star}
,
and for
v\in T_{\Omega_{\star}}\mathcal{M}
,

\Pi_{\exp_{\Omega_{\star}}(v)}=\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}+D\Pi_{\Omega_{\star}}[v]+o(\|v\|).

Define, for any

M\in\mathcal{S}_{+}^{d}
with
\rank(M)=k
, the
whitened descriptor

B(M):=\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}^{1/2}\,M\,\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{S}_{+}^{d}.

Let
\Pi(B)
denote the Euclidean orthogonal projector onto
\mathrm{range}(B)
. Then the induced matrix in (
F.27
) admits the

M
–only representation

P(M)=\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}^{-1/2}\,\Pi\!\big(B(M)\big)\,\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}^{-1/2}=\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}^{-1/2}\,B(M)\,B(M)^{+}\,\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}^{-1/2}.

(F.29)

For the fixed target
f_{\star}
, define
m_{\star}:=\mathbb{E}[f_{\star}(X)X]\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Since
m_{\star}
is
independent of
M
, we have for any tangent direction
v\in T_{M_{\star}}\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
,

\displaystyle D\Pi_{M_{\star}}[v]\,f_{\star}(x)

\displaystyle=x^{\top}DP(M_{\star})[v]\;m_{\star},

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}(v)f_{\star})(x)

\displaystyle:=-D\Pi_{M_{\star}}[v]\,f_{\star}(x)=-\,x^{\top}DP(M_{\star})[v]\;m_{\star}.

(F.30)

Derivative of
P(M)
.

Let
B_{\star}:=B(M_{\star})=\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}^{1/2}M_{\star}\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}^{1/2}
and

\dot{B}:=DB(M_{\star})[v]=\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}^{1/2}v\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}^{1/2}
.
Differentiating (
F.29
) yields

DP(M_{\star})[v]=\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}^{-1/2}\,D\Pi(B_{\star})[\dot{B}]\;\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}^{-1/2}.

(F.31)

Assume an eigengap at
k
for
B_{\star}
, so that
\Pi(\cdot)
is Fréchet differentiable at

B_{\star}
. Let
B_{\star}=U\mathrm{diag}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})U^{\top}
with

U=[U_{1},U_{2}]
,
\Lambda_{1}=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k})
,

\Lambda_{2}=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{k+1},\ldots,\lambda_{d})
, and

\min_{i\leqslant k<j}|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}|>0
.

Lemma F.15
.

For any symmetric direction

\dot{B}\in\mathcal{S}^{d}
,

D\Pi(B_{\star})[\dot{B}]=U_{2}\,(G\oslash\Delta)\,U_{1}^{\top}+U_{1}\,\big(G^{\top}\oslash\Delta^{\top}\big)\,U_{2}^{\top},

(F.32)

where

G:=U_{2}^{\top}\dot{B}\,U_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{(d-k)\times k},\qquad\Delta_{ai}:=\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k+a},

and
\oslash
is the elementwise division.

Proof.

We work in the eigenbasis of
B_{\star}
, i.e. replace
\dot{B}
by

\widetilde{\dot{B}}:=U^{\top}\dot{B}\,U
and write the projector in this basis.
Since
\Pi(B)
is orthogonally equivariant, it suffices to prove the claim for

B_{\star}=\mathrm{diag}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})
, in which case
\Pi(B_{\star})=P_{\star}:=\mathrm{diag}(I_{k},0)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a positively oriented contour in the complex plane enclosing

\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k}\}
and excluding
\{\lambda_{k+1},\ldots,\lambda_{d}\}
.
By the Riesz projector formula,

\Pi(B)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma}(zI-B)^{-1}\,dz

(F.33)

for all
B
in a neighborhood of
B_{\star}
. Differentiating (
F.33
) at
B_{\star}

in direction
\dot{B}
and using the standard resolvent identity

D\big((zI-B)^{-1}\big)\Big|_{B=B_{\star}}[\dot{B}]=(zI-B_{\star})^{-1}\,\dot{B}\,(zI-B_{\star})^{-1},

we obtain

D\Pi(B_{\star})[\dot{B}]=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma}(zI-B_{\star})^{-1}\,\dot{B}\,(zI-B_{\star})^{-1}\,dz.

(F.34)

Since
B_{\star}=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{d})
is diagonal in this basis,

(zI-B_{\star})^{-1}
is also diagonal with entries
(z-\lambda_{j})^{-1}
. Therefore,
the
(p,q)
entry of the integrand in (
F.34
) equals

\big[(zI-B_{\star})^{-1}\,\dot{B}\,(zI-B_{\star})^{-1}\big]_{pq}=\frac{\dot{B}_{pq}}{(z-\lambda_{p})(z-\lambda_{q})}.

Hence

\big[D\Pi(B_{\star})[\dot{B}]\big]_{pq}=\dot{B}_{pq}\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma}\frac{dz}{(z-\lambda_{p})(z-\lambda_{q})}.

(F.35)

Now consider cases.

(i)

p,q\leqslant k
: If both
\lambda_{p}
and
\lambda_{q}
lie inside of the contour
\Gamma
, then we have

\displaystyle I_{pq}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma}\frac{dz}{(z-\lambda_{p})(z-\lambda_{q})}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma}\frac{1}{\lambda_{p}-\lambda_{q}}(\frac{1}{z-\lambda_{p}}-\frac{1}{z-\lambda_{q}})dz

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i(\lambda_{p}-\lambda_{q})}(1-1)=0.

(ii)

p,q>k
: If both
\lambda_{p}
and
\lambda_{q}
lie outside of the contour
\Gamma
, then the integrand in (
F.35
) is holomorphic inside
\Gamma
and the contour integral vanishes.

(iii)

p\leqslant k<q
: Then
\lambda_{p}
is inside
\Gamma
and
\lambda_{q}
is outside, so the integrand in (
F.35
) has a single pole inside
\Gamma
at
z=\lambda_{p}
with residue
1/(\lambda_{p}-\lambda_{q})
. Therefore

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma}\frac{dz}{(z-\lambda_{p})(z-\lambda_{q})}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{p}-\lambda_{q}},

and thus

\big[D\Pi(B_{\star})[\dot{B}]\big]_{pq}=\frac{\dot{B}_{pq}}{\lambda_{p}-\lambda_{q}}.

By symmetry, the case
q\leqslant k<p
gives the transpose.

Writing this in block form with respect to the split
\mathbb{R}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{k}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{d-k}
yields

D\Pi(B_{\star})[\dot{B}]=\begin{pmatrix}0&H^{\top}\\
H&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad H_{ai}=\frac{\dot{B}_{k+a,i}}{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k+a}}.

Returning to the original basis (undoing the
U
-conjugation) gives (
F.32
)
with
G:=U_{2}^{\top}\dot{B}\,U_{1}
and
\Delta_{ai}:=\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k+a}
.
∎

Pretraining fluctuations.

We will derive the asymptotic distribution of
m\lVert\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{log}_{M_{\star}}(\hat{M}_{m})\rVert
.

\displaystyle m\lVert\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{log}_{M_{\star}}(\hat{M}_{m}))\rVert_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert x^{\top}DP(M_{\star})[\sqrt{m}\mathrm{log}_{M_{\star}}(\hat{M}_{m})]\;m_{\star}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\beta_{\star}^{\top}A_{\star}\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}DP(M_{\star})[\sqrt{m}\mathrm{log}_{M_{\star}}(\hat{M}_{m})]^{T}DP(M_{\star})[\sqrt{m}\mathrm{log}_{M_{\star}}(\hat{M}_{m})]\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}A_{\star}^{\top}\beta_{\star}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\beta_{\star}^{\top}A_{\star}\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}DP(M_{\star})[Z]^{T}DP(M_{\star})[Z]\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}A_{\star}^{\top}\beta_{\star}

(F.36)

where the exact form of
DP(M_{\star})
is calculated in
Equation

F.31
and (
F.32
), and
Z
is a mean-zero Gaussian with the covariance
V_{\star}
(
Equation

F.26
).

F.9
Comparison to
6

We now study the following concrete example to compare our asymptotic result with the general upper bound of
6
. Assume that
\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}=I_{d}
and

\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}=\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}&0\\
0&\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}=\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{+}&0\\
0&\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},2}^{+}\end{pmatrix}

with

\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}=\operatorname{diag}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k}),\qquad\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},2}=\operatorname{diag}(b_{1},\dots,b_{d-k}),

and

\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{+}=\operatorname{diag}(c_{1},\dots,c_{k}),\qquad\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},2}^{+}=\operatorname{diag}(e_{1},\dots,e_{d-k}).

Furthermore, we assume that all diagonal entries are strictly positive and

\frac{c_{i}}{a_{i}}>\frac{e_{j}}{b_{j}},\qquad\forall\,i\in\{1,\dots,k\},\ \forall\,j\in\{1,\dots,d-k\}.

In this case, the whitened cross-covariance matrix
C=\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{-1/2}

is diagonal and given by

C=\operatorname{diag}\!\left(\frac{c_{1}}{a_{1}},\dots,\frac{c_{k}}{a_{k}},\frac{e_{1}}{b_{1}},\dots,\frac{e_{d-k}}{b_{d-k}}\right).

By the ordering assumption, the top-
k
eigenvalues of
C
are
\frac{c_{1}}{a_{1}},\dots,\frac{c_{k}}{a_{k}}
, so the constrained population minimizer over
\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
is

M_{\star}=\begin{pmatrix}R&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}

where
R:=\operatorname{diag}(r_{1},\dots,r_{k})
and
r_{i}:=\frac{c_{i}}{a_{i}^{2}}
. Any tangent matrix
H\in T_{M_{\star}}\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
has the block form

H=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
B^{\top}&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad A\in\mathbb{S}^{k},\quad B\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times(d-k)}.

Let
\mu_{i}\coloneqq\frac{e_{i}}{b_{i}}
,
\lambda_{i}\coloneqq\frac{c_{i}}{a_{i}}
, and
\tau\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}^{2}
.
By Corollary (
F.26
), the covariance operator
V_{\star}
is given by

\langle H_{1},V_{\star}H_{2}\rangle=C_{aa}(H_{1}^{\prime},H_{2}^{\prime})-C_{ab}(H_{1}^{\prime},H_{2}^{\prime})-C_{ab}(H_{2}^{\prime},H_{1}^{\prime})+C_{bb}(H_{1}^{\prime},H_{2}^{\prime}),

where

H_{\ell}^{\prime}=\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{-1}H_{\ell}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{-1}A_{\ell}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{-1}&\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{-1}B_{\ell}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},2}^{-1}\\
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},2}^{-1}B_{\ell}^{\top}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}.

We now compute the three terms in (
F.15
)–(
(iii)
). Using the block-diagonal form of
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}
, a direct multiplication gives

H_{1}^{\prime}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}=\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{-1}A_{1}&\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{-1}B_{1}\\
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},2}^{-1}B_{1}^{\top}&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad H_{2}^{\prime}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}=\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{-1}A_{2}&\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{-1}B_{2}\\
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},2}^{-1}B_{2}^{\top}&0\end{pmatrix}.

Hence

\tr(H_{1}^{\prime}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{2}^{\prime}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})=\tr(\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{-1}A_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{-1}A_{2})+2\tr(\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{-1}B_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},2}^{-1}B_{2}^{\top}).

Similarly,

\tr(H_{1}^{\prime}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}H_{2}^{\prime}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+})=\tr(\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{-1}A_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{+}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{-1}A_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{+})+2\tr(\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{-1}B_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},2}^{+}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},2}^{-1}B_{2}^{\top}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{+}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{-1}).

Since all these matrices are diagonal, this becomes entrywise

\displaystyle C_{aa}(H_{1}^{\prime},H_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\frac{1+\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}}{a_{i}a_{j}}\,(A_{1})_{ij}(A_{2})_{ij}+2\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{d-k}\frac{1+\lambda_{i}\mu_{j}}{a_{i}b_{j}}\,(B_{1})_{ij}(B_{2})_{ij}.

Since
M_{\star}=\begin{pmatrix}R\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
, we obtain

P_{\ell}=M_{\star}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{\ell}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}RA_{\ell}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}^{-1}&RB_{\ell}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},2}^{-1}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}.

Then

\displaystyle C_{ab}(H_{1}^{\prime},H_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\tr(P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{1}^{\prime}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+})+\tr(P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}H_{1}^{\prime}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\frac{r_{i}(\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j})}{a_{j}}\,(A_{1})_{ij}(A_{2})_{ij}+2\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{d-k}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}}{a_{i}b_{j}}\,(B_{1})_{ij}(B_{2})_{ij}.

By symmetry,

\displaystyle C_{ab}(H_{2}^{\prime},H_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\frac{r_{j}(\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j})}{a_{i}}\,(A_{1})_{ij}(A_{2})_{ij}+2\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{d-k}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}}{a_{i}b_{j}}\,(B_{1})_{ij}(B_{2})_{ij}.

We have

Q:=M_{\star}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}M_{\star}=\begin{pmatrix}R\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre},1}R&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},

so that
\tr(Q\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}^{2}=\tau
. Also,
\tr(P_{\ell}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}(A_{\ell})_{ii}
. Further,

\tr(P_{1}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}P_{2}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})=\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}r_{i}r_{j}\,(A_{1})_{ij}(A_{2})_{ij}.

Finally, using again that all matrices are diagonal in block form,

\tr(H_{1}^{\prime}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H_{2}^{\prime}\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}Q\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}})=\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}}{a_{i}a_{j}}\,(A_{1})_{ij}(A_{2})_{ij}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{d-k}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}}{a_{i}b_{j}}\,(B_{1})_{ij}(B_{2})_{ij}.

Substituting into (
(iii)
), we obtain

\displaystyle C_{bb}(H_{1}^{\prime},H_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}(A_{1})_{ii}\Big)\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{k}r_{j}(A_{2})_{jj}\Big)

\displaystyle+\tau\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{a_{i}a_{j}}(A_{1})_{ij}(A_{2})_{ij}+2\tau\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{d-k}\frac{1}{a_{i}b_{j}}(B_{1})_{ij}(B_{2})_{ij}

\displaystyle+4\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}}{a_{i}a_{j}}(A_{1})_{ij}(A_{2})_{ij}+4\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{d-k}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}}{a_{i}b_{j}}(B_{1})_{ij}(B_{2})_{ij}.

Substituting the previous expressions into Corollary (
F.26
) yields

\displaystyle\langle H_{1},V_{\star}H_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}(A_{1})_{ii}\Big)\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{k}r_{j}(A_{2})_{jj}\Big)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1+\tau+3\lambda_{i}^{2}}{a_{i}^{2}}\,(A_{1})_{ii}(A_{2})_{ii}

\displaystyle\quad+2\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant k}\frac{1+\tau+\lambda_{i}^{2}+\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}+\lambda_{j}^{2}}{a_{i}a_{j}}\,(A_{1})_{ij}(A_{2})_{ij}+2\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{d-k}\frac{1+\tau+\lambda_{i}^{2}}{a_{i}b_{j}}\,(B_{1})_{ij}(B_{2})_{ij}.

Therefore, if

Z=\begin{pmatrix}Z_{11}&Z_{12}\\
Z_{12}^{\top}&0\end{pmatrix}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,V_{\star}),

then the covariance structure is as follows:

(i)

The diagonal entries of
Z_{11}
are jointly Gaussian with covariance

\operatorname{Diag}\!\left(\frac{1+\tau+3\lambda_{i}^{2}}{a_{i}^{2}}\right)_{i=1}^{k}+rr^{\top},\qquad r=(r_{1},\dots,r_{k})^{\top}.

(ii)

The off-diagonal entries
(Z_{11})_{ij}
,
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant k
, are independent with variances

\frac{2(1+\tau+\lambda_{i}^{2}+\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}+\lambda_{j}^{2})}{a_{i}a_{j}}.

(iii)

The entries of
Z_{12}
are independent with variances

\frac{2(1+\tau+\lambda_{i}^{2})}{a_{i}b_{j}}.

We now specialize (
F.8
) to this setting. In this case,
B(M)=\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}^{1/2}M\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}^{1/2}=M
so that the derivative of the spectral projector simplifies to

DP(M_{\star})[H]=D\Pi(M_{\star})[H].

By
Equation

F.32
, the Fréchet derivative of the projector at
M_{\star}
in direction
Z
is

DP(M_{\star})[Z]=\begin{pmatrix}0&R^{-1}Z_{12}\\
Z_{12}^{\top}R^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}.

Consequently,

DP(M_{\star})[Z]^{\top}DP(M_{\star})[Z]=\begin{pmatrix}R^{-1}Z_{12}Z_{12}^{\top}R^{-1}&0\\
0&Z_{12}^{\top}R^{-2}Z_{12}\end{pmatrix}.

Substituting this into (
F.8
) yields

L:=\beta_{\star}^{\top}A_{\star}\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}DP(M_{\star})[Z]^{\top}DP(M_{\star})[Z]\Sigma_{\mathrm{down}}A_{\star}^{\top}\beta_{\star}=\beta_{\star}^{\top}A_{\star}DP(M_{\star})[Z]^{\top}DP(M_{\star})[Z]A_{\star}^{\top}\beta_{\star}.

Writing

m_{\star}:=A_{\star}^{\top}\beta_{\star}=\begin{pmatrix}m_{1}\\
m_{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad m_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\quad m_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-k},

we obtain

L=m_{1}^{\top}R^{-1}Z_{12}Z_{12}^{\top}R^{-1}m_{1}+m_{2}^{\top}Z_{12}^{\top}R^{-2}Z_{12}m_{2}.

In particular, in the aligned basis where
m_{\star}
lies entirely in the leading
k
-dimensional eigenspace,
m_{\star}=\begin{pmatrix}R^{1/2}\beta_{\star}\\
0\end{pmatrix}
, the second term vanishes and therefore

L=\beta_{\star}^{\top}R^{-1/2}Z_{12}Z_{12}^{\top}R^{-1/2}\beta_{\star}=\|Z_{12}^{\top}R^{-1/2}\beta_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{d-k}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\beta_{\star,i}}{\sqrt{r_{i}}}(Z_{12})_{ij}\right)^{2}.

(F.37)

By the explicit covariance structure of
V_{\star}
, the entries of
Z_{12}
are independent centered Gaussians with variances

\operatorname{Var}\bigl((Z_{12})_{ij}\bigr)=\frac{2(1+\tau+\lambda_{i}^{2})}{a_{i}b_{j}},\qquad 1\leqslant i\leqslant k,\quad 1\leqslant j\leqslant d-k.

Hence, for each
j
,

Y_{j}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\beta_{\star,i}}{\sqrt{r_{i}}}(Z_{12})_{ij}

is centered Gaussian with variance

\operatorname{Var}(Y_{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\beta_{\star,i}^{2}}{r_{i}}\,\frac{2(1+\tau+\lambda_{i}^{2})}{a_{i}b_{j}}.

Using
r_{i}=\lambda_{i}/a_{i}
, this simplifies to

\operatorname{Var}(Y_{j})=\frac{2}{b_{j}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\beta_{\star,i}^{2}\frac{(1+\tau+\lambda_{i}^{2})}{\lambda_{i}}.

Therefore,
L=\sum_{j=1}^{d-k}Y_{j}^{2}
is a weighted chi-square random variable, namely

L\overset{d}{=}\sum_{j=1}^{d-k}\sigma_{j}^{2}\chi_{j}^{2}(1),\qquad\sigma_{j}^{2}=\frac{2}{b_{j}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\beta_{\star,i}^{2}\frac{(1+\tau+\lambda_{i}^{2})}{\lambda_{i}},

(F.38)

where the
\chi_{j}^{2}(1)
are independent chi-square random variables with one degree of freedom.

Proof of
Corollary

6.3
.

The result follows by a direct substitution into the general expression for the pretraining interaction term given in
Equation

F.38
.

Under the diagonal model specified in Section 6.3, we have

b_{j}=1,\qquad\beta_{\star,i}=1,\qquad\lambda_{i}=\frac{1}{i},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tau=\sum_{j=1}^{k}j^{-2}.

Substituting these quantities into
Equation

F.38
, we obtain

\sigma_{j}^{2}=2\sum_{i=1}^{k}i\bigl(1+i^{-2}+\tau\bigr),

which does not depend on
j
. Therefore, the weighted chi-square random variable reduces to

L=\left(2\sum_{i=1}^{k}i\bigl(1+i^{-2}+\tau\bigr)\right)\sum_{j=1}^{d-k}\chi_{j}^{2}(1)=\left(2\sum_{i=1}^{k}i\bigl(1+i^{-2}+\tau\bigr)\right)\chi_{(d-k)}^{2},

establishing the stated convergence in distribution.

The scaling of the expectation follows immediately from the above expression, yielding the claimed order.

∎

Pretraining asymptotic sub-optimality.

Let

\mathcal{Q}:=\frac{1}{2}\bigl\langle Z,\,2\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}Z\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}\bigr\rangle_{F}=\bigl\langle Z,\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}Z\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}\bigr\rangle_{F}.

We note that the population Hessian operator at
M_{\star}
is given by the map
H\mapsto\langle H,2\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}H\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}\rangle
. A second-order Taylor expansion of
L_{\mathrm{spec}}
around
M_{\star}
, together with the first-order optimality condition, yields

m\bigl(L_{\mathrm{spec}}(\hat{M}_{m})-L_{\mathrm{spec}}(M_{\star})\bigr)\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\;\mathcal{Q}.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}a_{i}a_{j}(Z_{11})_{ij}^{2}+2\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{u=1}^{d-k}a_{i}b_{u}(Z_{12})_{iu}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}^{2}(Z_{11})_{ii}^{2}+2\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant k}a_{i}a_{j}(Z_{11})_{ij}^{2}+2\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{u=1}^{d-k}a_{i}b_{u}(Z_{12})_{iu}^{2}.

We now compute the law of each term.

Let

z_{\mathrm{diag}}:=\bigl((Z_{11})_{11},\dots,(Z_{11})_{kk}\bigr)^{\top}.

By assumption,

z_{\mathrm{diag}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,K),\qquad K=\operatorname{Diag}\!\left(\frac{1+\tau+3\lambda_{i}^{2}}{a_{i}^{2}}\right)_{i=1}^{k}+rr^{\top},\qquad r_{i}=\frac{\lambda_{i}}{a_{i}}.

Hence

\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}^{2}(Z_{11})_{ii}^{2}=z_{\mathrm{diag}}^{\top}\operatorname{Diag}(a_{1}^{2},\dots,a_{k}^{2})\,z_{\mathrm{diag}}.

If
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{k})
, this quadratic form may be written as

z_{\mathrm{diag}}^{\top}\operatorname{Diag}(a_{1}^{2},\dots,a_{k}^{2})\,z_{\mathrm{diag}}\;\overset{d}{=}\;\xi^{\top}M\xi,

where

M=\operatorname{Diag}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\,K\,\operatorname{Diag}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})=\operatorname{Diag}(1+\tau+3\lambda_{i}^{2})_{i=1}^{k}+\lambda\lambda^{\top},

with
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k})^{\top}
. Therefore, if

\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{k}
denote the eigenvalues of
M
, then

\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}^{2}(Z_{11})_{ii}^{2}\;\overset{d}{=}\;\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\rho_{\ell}\chi_{\ell}^{2}(1).

For
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant k
, the variables
(Z_{11})_{ij}
are independent centered
Gaussians with variance

\operatorname{Var}\bigl((Z_{11})_{ij}\bigr)=\frac{2(1+\tau+\lambda_{i}^{2}+\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}+\lambda_{j}^{2})}{a_{i}a_{j}}.

Hence

2a_{i}a_{j}(Z_{11})_{ij}^{2}\;\overset{d}{=}\;4\bigl(1+\tau+\lambda_{i}^{2}+\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}+\lambda_{j}^{2}\bigr)\chi_{ij}^{2}(1),

where the
\chi_{ij}^{2}(1)
are independent.

For
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
and
1\leqslant j\leqslant d-k
, the variables
(Z_{12})_{ij}
are
independent centered Gaussians with variance

\operatorname{Var}\bigl((Z_{12})_{ij}\bigr)=\frac{2(1+\tau+\lambda_{i}^{2})}{a_{i}b_{j}}.

Therefore

2a_{i}b_{j}(Z_{12})_{ij}^{2}\;\overset{d}{=}\;4(1+\tau+\lambda_{i}^{2})\widetilde{\chi}_{ij}^{2}(1),

where the
\widetilde{\chi}_{ij}^{2}(1)
are independent. Using Gaussian independence of the orthogonal coordinates, we obtain

\mathcal{Q}\;\overset{d}{=}\;\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\rho_{\ell}\chi_{\ell}^{2}(1)+\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant k}4\bigl(1+\tau+\lambda_{i}^{2}+\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}+\lambda_{j}^{2}\bigr)\chi_{ij}^{2}(1)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{d-k}4(1+\tau+\lambda_{i}^{2})\widetilde{\chi}_{ij}^{2}(1),

(F.39)

where
\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{k}
are the eigenvalues of

M=\operatorname{Diag}(1+\tau+3\lambda_{i}^{2})_{i=1}^{k}+\lambda\lambda^{\top}.

Taking expectations yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Q}]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}(M)+4\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant k}\bigl(1+\tau+\lambda_{i}^{2}+\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}+\lambda_{j}^{2}\bigr)+4\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{u=1}^{d-k}(1+\tau+\lambda_{i}^{2})

\displaystyle=k(4d-2k-1)(1+\tau)+2(2d-1)\tau+2(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i})^{2}.

(F.40)

Connection to
6
.

We compare our result to the generalization bound of
6
. In their result, the contribution of pretraining appears through a product of a conditioning factor and a
sub-optimality term, of the form

\|T_{\lambda}^{-1}\Pi_{\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}}f_{\star}\|^{2}\bigl(L_{\mathrm{spec}}(\hat{M})-L_{\mathrm{spec}}(M_{\star})\bigr).

We now show how the conditioning factor
\|T_{\lambda}^{-1}\Pi_{\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}}f_{\star}\|^{2}
reduces in our setting.

In the present model with
\lambda=0
, the operator
T_{\lambda}

coincides with the covariance matrix
C=\operatorname{diag}(R,0)
. Moreover, the projection onto the feature space is given by

\Pi_{\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}}=\operatorname{diag}(I_{k},0).

Since the target function satisfies
f_{\star}(x)=\beta_{\star}^{\top}A_{\star}x
,
its projection onto the feature space depends only on the first
k

coordinates. Therefore, the action of
T_{\lambda}^{-1}\Pi_{\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}}

amounts to inverting
R
on the top-
k
subspace, yielding

\|T_{\lambda}^{-1}\Pi_{\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}}f_{\star}\|^{2}=\|C^{-1}A_{\star}^{\top}\beta_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}=\|R^{-\frac{1}{2}}\beta_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}.

Consequently,

m\|T_{\lambda}^{-1}\Pi_{\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}}f_{\star}\|^{2}\bigl(L_{\mathrm{spec}}(\hat{M})-L_{\mathrm{spec}}(M_{\star})\bigr)\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\;\|R^{-\frac{1}{2}}\beta_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathcal{Q}.

In the setting where
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}=I_{d}
,
\Sigma_{\mathrm{pre}}^{+}=\diag(1,1/2,\ldots,1/d)
, and
\beta_{\star}=(1,\cdots,1)^{\top}
, we have

\|R^{-\frac{1}{2}}\beta_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}i=\Theta(k^{2}),

while

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Q}]=k(4d-2k-1)(1+\tau)+2(2d-1)\tau+2(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i})^{2}=\Theta\big(k(d-k)\big).

Consequently, the expected pretraining contribution satisfies

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|R^{-\frac{1}{2}}\beta_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathcal{Q}\right]=\|R^{-\frac{1}{2}}\beta_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Q}]=O\big(k^{3}(d-k)\big).

Thus, the pretraining contribution scales as
O(k^{3}(d-k))
in this model.

Appendix G
Proofs of Section
6.2

In this subsection, we derive the exact maximum likelihood estimator of the
quotient-level parameter

M\coloneqq AA^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},

introduced in Section
6.2
.
Recall the unlabeled pretraining model

X=A_{\star}Z+\mu,\qquad Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{k}),\quad\mu\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),

(G.1)

with
Z
and
\mu
independent and
k\ll d
. By marginalizing over the latent factor
Z
, the pretraining covariate
X

is Gaussian with covariance

X\sim\mathcal{N}\bigl(0,\;\Sigma_{\star}\bigr),\qquad\Sigma_{\star}=A_{\star}A_{\star}^{\top}+I_{d}=M_{\star}+I_{d},

(G.2)

where
M_{\star}=A_{\star}A_{\star}^{\top}
is a rank-
k
positive semidefinite matrix.

The negative log-likelihood of the pretraining sample

\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
as a function of
M\succcurlyeq 0
is

\ell_{m}(M)=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\log\det(I_{d}+M)+\tr\!\big(S_{m}(I_{d}+M)^{-1}\big)\Big),

(G.3)

up to an additive constant independent of
M
. Here, we defined
S_{m}\coloneqq\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}
. This maximum likelihood problem is exactly
probabilistic principal component analysis
(PPCA); see
35
.

Let
S_{m}=U\Lambda U^{\top}
be the eigenvalue decomposition of the sample covariance, and
\Lambda=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{d})
where
\lambda_{1}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{d}
. Let
U_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
be the
k
-dimensional leading eigenspace.

Lemma G.1

(PPCA maximum likelihood estimator for
M
)
.

For the model
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}+M_{\star})
with
\mathrm{rank}(M_{\star})=k
, a maximum likelihood estimator is

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{m}\;=\;U_{k}\bigl(\mathrm{diag}((\lambda_{1}-1)_{+}\cdots,(\lambda_{k}-1)_{+})\bigr)U_{k}^{\top}.

(G.4)

Proof.

See
35
.
∎

Consistency.

Next, we will prove the maximum likelihood estimator in Equation
G.4
is consistent.

Proposition G.2

(Consistency of the quotient MLE)
.

The estimator in Equation (
G.4
) is consistent, that is,
\hat{M}_{m}\to M_{\star}
almost surely (hence also in probability) in Frobenius norm.

Proof.

By the strong law of large numbers,
S_{m}\to\Sigma_{\star}
almost surely in operator norm. Fix an outcome in this
almost-sure event and write
\Delta_{m}\coloneqq S_{m}-\Sigma_{\star}
, so
\|\Delta_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\to 0
.
Let
P_{\star}
denote the orthogonal projector onto the top-
k
eigenspace of
\Sigma_{\star}
and let

\hat{P}_{m}\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{k}\hat{u}_{j}\hat{u}_{j}^{\top}

be the projector onto the top-
k
eigenspace of
S_{m}
.
By Davis-Kahan’s
\sin\Theta
theorem (
10
), for all
m
large enough,

\|\hat{P}_{m}-P_{\star}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\frac{2\|\Delta_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{\lambda_{k}(\Sigma_{\star})-\lambda_{k+1}(\Sigma_{\star})}=\frac{2\|\Delta_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{\lambda_{k}(\Sigma_{\star})-1}\;\xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{}\;0.

(G.5)

In particular,
\hat{P}_{m}\to P_{\star}
in operator norm, hence also in Frobenius norm.

Let
\gamma\coloneqq\lambda_{k}(\Sigma_{\star})-1>0
denote the eigengap. By Weyl’s inequality,

|\hat{\lambda}_{i}-\lambda_{i}(\Sigma_{\star})|\leqslant\|\Delta_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\qquad\text{for all }i\in[d].

Hence, for all
m
large enough so that
\|\Delta_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\gamma/2
, we get

\hat{\lambda}_{k}\geqslant\lambda_{k}(\Sigma_{\star})-\|\Delta_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geqslant 1+\gamma/2,

and therefore
\hat{\lambda}_{i}\geqslant\hat{\lambda}_{k}>1
for all
i\leqslant k
. In particular,
\hat{\lambda}_{i}-1>0
for
i\leqslant k
. Next, define the rank-
k
truncation map
\Pi_{k}(\cdot)
by

\Pi_{k}(B)\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}(B)\,u_{j}(B)u_{j}(B)^{\top},

where
\lambda_{1}(B)\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{d}(B)
are the eigenvalues of
B
and
u_{j}(B)
are corresponding orthonormal
eigenvectors. Then
\Pi_{k}(S_{m})=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\hat{\lambda}_{j}\hat{u}_{j}\hat{u}_{j}^{\top}
and

\hat{M}_{m}=\Pi_{k}(S_{m})-\hat{P}_{m}.

Similarly, since
\lambda_{k+1}(\Sigma_{\star})=\cdots=\lambda_{d}(\Sigma_{\star})=1
and
\Sigma_{\star}\succcurlyeq 0
,

\Pi_{k}(\Sigma_{\star})=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}(\Sigma_{\star})u_{j}u_{j}^{\top}=M_{\star}+P_{\star},\qquad\text{so that}\qquad M_{\star}=\Pi_{k}(\Sigma_{\star})-P_{\star}.

Therefore,

\hat{M}_{m}-M_{\star}=\bigl(\Pi_{k}(S_{m})-\Pi_{k}(\Sigma_{\star})\bigr)-\bigl(\hat{P}_{m}-P_{\star}\bigr).

(G.6)

We now control the two terms. For the projector term we already have
\|\hat{P}_{m}-P_{\star}\|_{F}\to 0
by (
G.5
).
For the truncated part, note that for any symmetric
B,B^{\prime}
,

\|\Pi_{k}(B)-\Pi_{k}(B^{\prime})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant k\,\|B-B^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{op}},

since
\Pi_{k}(B)-\Pi_{k}(B^{\prime})=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\bigl(\lambda_{j}(B)u_{j}(B)u_{j}(B)^{\top}-\lambda_{j}(B^{\prime})u_{j}(B^{\prime})u_{j}(B^{\prime})^{\top}\bigr)
and each
rank-one term has operator norm bounded by
|\lambda_{j}(B)-\lambda_{j}(B^{\prime})|+\|B-B^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
, while

|\lambda_{j}(B)-\lambda_{j}(B^{\prime})|\leqslant\|B-B^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
by Weyl’s inequality. Consequently,

\|\Pi_{k}(S_{m})-\Pi_{k}(\Sigma_{\star})\|_{F}\leqslant\sqrt{k}\,\|\Pi_{k}(S_{m})-\Pi_{k}(\Sigma_{\star})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant k^{3/2}\,\|S_{m}-\Sigma_{\star}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=k^{3/2}\,\|\Delta_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\to 0.

Combining with (
G.6
) yields
\|\hat{M}_{m}-M_{\star}\|_{F}\to 0
along the fixed almost-sure outcome. This proves

\hat{M}_{m}\to M_{\star}
almost surely.
∎

Asymptotic Normality

Finally, we will derive the asymptotic distribution of
\sqrt{m}(\hat{M}_{m}-M_{\star})
. Let
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{x})
with

\Sigma_{x}=I_{d}+M_{\star},\qquad\operatorname{rank}(M_{\star})=k.

First, we write the eigendecomposition

\Sigma_{x}=U_{\star}\begin{pmatrix}I_{k}+D&0\\
0&I_{d-k}\end{pmatrix}U_{\star}^{\top},\qquad D=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}),\ \ \sigma_{k}>0,

so
M_{\star}=U_{\star}\begin{pmatrix}D&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}U_{\star}^{\top}
.
Let the sample covariance be
S_{m}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}
.
Recall the (fixed-
k
) PPCA MLE can be written as

\hat{M}_{m}=\hat{U}_{k}\,\operatorname{diag}(\hat{\lambda}_{1}-1,\dots,\hat{\lambda}_{k}-1)\,\hat{U}_{k}^{\top},

where
\hat{\lambda}_{1}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\hat{\lambda}_{d}
and
\hat{U}_{k}
are the top-
k
eigenpairs of
S_{m}
.
Since
\lambda_{k}(\Sigma_{x})=1+\sigma_{k}>1
, we have
\hat{\lambda}_{i}>1
for
i\leqslant k
with probability
\to 1
,
so the “
(\cdot)_{+}
” truncation is asymptotically inactive and the map is smooth at
\Sigma_{x}
.

The Gaussian fourth-moment identity gives

\sqrt{m}\,\mathrm{vec}(S_{m}-\Sigma_{x})\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\ \mathcal{N}\!\big(0,\ (I+K)(\Sigma_{x}\otimes\Sigma_{x})\big),

where
K
is the commutation matrix.

Define the smooth map (near
\Sigma_{x}
)

g(\Sigma)\coloneqq\text{``best rank-$k$ PSD approximation of }\Sigma-I_{d}\text{''},\qquad\hat{M}_{m}=g(S_{m}),\quad M_{\star}=g(\Sigma_{x}).

Then

Z_{m}\coloneqq\sqrt{m}\,(\hat{M}_{m}-M_{\star})\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\ Z,\qquad Z=Dg_{\Sigma_{x}}[G],

where
G
is the Gaussian matrix limit of
\sqrt{m}(S_{m}-\Sigma_{x})
.

Let
U_{\star}=[U_{1}\ U_{2}]
with
U_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
spanning the signal subspace and

U_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(d-k)}
spanning its orthogonal complement, and rotate

\tilde{G}\coloneqq U_{\star}^{\top}GU_{\star}=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{G}_{11}&\tilde{G}_{12}\\
\tilde{G}_{21}&\tilde{G}_{22}\end{pmatrix}.

A first-order perturbation calculation (using the eigengap
\sigma_{k}>0
) yields the simple derivative

Dg_{\Sigma_{x}}[G]=U_{\star}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{G}_{11}&\tilde{G}_{12}\\
\tilde{G}_{21}&0\end{pmatrix}U_{\star}^{\top}.

Therefore the asymptotic fluctuation has the explicit form

Z=U_{\star}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{G}_{11}&\tilde{G}_{12}\\
\tilde{G}_{21}&0\end{pmatrix}U_{\star}^{\top},\qquad\tilde{G}=U_{\star}^{\top}GU_{\star},

and in particular
Z
is mean-zero Gaussian (as a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d(d+1)/2}
).

If we define the linear operator
\mathcal{P}:\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
by

\mathcal{P}(Y)\coloneqq U_{\star}\begin{pmatrix}Y_{11}&Y_{12}\\
Y_{21}&0\end{pmatrix}U_{\star}^{\top}\quad\text{(blocks taken in the $U_{\star}$-basis),}

then
\mathrm{vec}(Z)=(\mathcal{P}\otimes I)\mathrm{vec}(G)
and hence

\mathrm{vec}(Z)\sim\mathcal{N}\!\big(0,\ (\mathcal{P}\otimes I)\,(I+K)(\Sigma_{x}\otimes\Sigma_{x})\,(\mathcal{P}\otimes I)^{\top}\big).

Equivalently, Let
P_{\star}
be the orthogonal projector onto
\operatorname{range}(M_{\star})
. Then, we have

\displaystyle Z\;=\;P_{\star}G+GP_{\star}-P_{\star}GP_{\star}.

(G.7)

Let
W\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
have i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries. Thus,

\displaystyle G\;\coloneqq\;\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\Sigma_{x}^{1/2}(W+W^{\top})\Sigma_{x}^{1/2}.

(G.8)

Substituting the expression for
G
gives the explicit representation of
Z

as a linear transform of a standard Gaussian matrix:

\displaystyle Z=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Big[P_{\star}\Sigma_{x}^{1/2}(W+W^{\top})\Sigma_{x}^{1/2}+\Sigma_{x}^{1/2}(W+W^{\top})\Sigma_{x}^{1/2}P_{\star}-P_{\star}\Sigma_{x}^{1/2}(W+W^{\top})\Sigma_{x}^{1/2}P_{\star}\Big].

(G.9)

Pretraining fluctuations.

Fix
\Sigma_{x}\coloneqq I_{d}+M_{\star}
and write
f_{\star}(x)=w_{\star}^{\top}x
with

w_{\star}=\Sigma_{x}^{-1}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}.

At
M_{\star}
, choose a local section with
s(M_{\star})=A_{\star}
and define

U_{\star}\coloneqq(I_{d}+M_{\star})^{-1}A_{\star}=\Sigma_{x}^{-1}A_{\star}.

Let
P_{\star}
be the
\Sigma_{x}
-orthogonal projector onto

\operatorname{range}(U_{\star})
:

P_{\star}=U_{\star}\big(U_{\star}^{\top}\Sigma_{x}U_{\star}\big)^{-1}U_{\star}^{\top}\Sigma_{x}.

Then
(\Pi_{M_{\star}}f_{\star})(x)=(P_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})}w_{\star})^{\top}x
and

P_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})}w_{\star}=w_{\star}
.

Recall
\mathcal{L}(v)\coloneqq-D\Pi_{M_{\star}}[v]\,f_{\star}
. Since

D\Pi_{M_{\star}}[v]\,f_{\star}(x)=(DP_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})}[v]\,w_{\star})^{\top}x
, we have

\mathcal{L}(v)(x)=-(DP_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})}[v]\,w_{\star})^{\top}x.

Define for each
M

U_{M}\coloneqq(I_{d}+M)^{-1}s(M)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k},\qquad\mathcal{S}_{M}\coloneqq\operatorname{range}(U_{M}),

and let
P_{M}^{(\Sigma_{x})}
denote the
\Sigma_{x}
-orthogonal projector onto
\mathcal{S}_{M}
.
By definition of an orthogonal projector onto
\mathcal{S}_{M}
, we have the matrix identity

P_{M}^{(\Sigma_{x})}U_{M}=U_{M}.

(G.10)

Fix
v\in T_{M_{\star}}\mathcal{M}_{d,k}
and consider a smooth curve
M(t)
in
\mathcal{M}_{d,k}

with
M(0)=M_{\star}
and
\dot{M}(0)=v
. Define
U(t)\coloneqq U_{M(t)}
and

P(t)\coloneqq P_{M(t)}^{(\Sigma_{x})}
. Then (
G.10
) becomes

P(t)\,U(t)=U(t)\qquad\text{for all $t$ near $0$.}

Differentiating at
t=0
and applying the product rule gives

\dot{P}(0)\,U_{\star}+P_{\star}\,\dot{U}(0)=\dot{U}(0),

where
U_{\star}\coloneqq U_{M_{\star}}
and
P_{\star}\coloneqq P_{M_{\star}}^{(\Sigma_{x})}
.
Rearranging yields the key identity

\dot{P}(0)\,U_{\star}=(I_{d}-P_{\star})\,\dot{U}(0).

(G.11)

Interpreting
\dot{P}(0)=DP_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})}[v]
and
\dot{U}(0)=DU_{\star}[v]
, we can rewrite
(
G.11
) as

DP_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})}[v]\;U_{\star}=(I_{d}-P_{\star})\;DU_{\star}[v].

(G.12)

Finally, since in our model
w_{\star}\in\mathcal{S}_{M_{\star}}
we can write

w_{\star}=U_{\star}\beta_{\star}

for the same
\beta_{\star}
appearing in
f_{\star}(x)=\beta_{\star}^{\top}U_{\star}^{\top}x
.
Multiplying (
G.12
) on the right by
\beta_{\star}
gives

DP_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})}[v]\;w_{\star}=DP_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})}[v]\;U_{\star}\beta_{\star}=(I_{d}-P_{\star})\;DU_{\star}[v]\;\beta_{\star}.

(G.13)

Moreover
U_{M}=(I_{d}+M)^{-1}s(M)
implies

DU_{\star}[v]\,\beta_{\star}=-\Sigma_{x}^{-1}v\,\Sigma_{x}^{-1}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}+\Sigma_{x}^{-1}Ds(M_{\star})[v]\,\beta_{\star}.

The section-dependent term does not change
\operatorname{range}(U_{M})
and is killed by

(I_{d}-P_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})})
.
Consequently,

DP_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})}[v]\,\Sigma_{x}^{-1}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}=-(I_{d}-P_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})})\Sigma_{x}^{-1}v\,\Sigma_{x}^{-1}A_{\star}\beta_{\star},

and therefore, for
v=Z
,

\mathcal{L}(Z)(x)=x^{\top}(I_{d}-P_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})})\Sigma_{x}^{-1}Z\,\Sigma_{x}^{-1}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}.

Define the coefficient vector

g(Z)\;\coloneqq\;(I_{d}-P_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})})\Sigma_{x}^{-1}Z\,\Sigma_{x}^{-1}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

so that
\mathcal{L}(Z)(x)=x^{\top}g(Z)
. Then

\|\mathcal{L}(Z)\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\big[(x^{\top}g(Z))^{2}\big]=g(Z)^{\top}\Sigma_{x}\,g(Z).

Equivalently, expanding
g(Z)
,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}(Z)\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big(\Sigma_{x}^{-1}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}\Big)^{\top}Z^{\top}\Sigma_{x}^{-1}\,(I_{d}-P_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})})^{\top}\Sigma_{x}(I_{d}-P_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})})\,\Sigma_{x}^{-1}Z\Big(\Sigma_{x}^{-1}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}\Big).

Since
P_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})}
is
\Sigma_{x}
-self-adjoint and idempotent,

(P_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})})^{\top}\Sigma_{x}=\Sigma_{x}P_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})},\qquad(P_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})})^{2}=P_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})},

we have the simplification

(I_{d}-P_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})})^{\top}\Sigma_{x}(I_{d}-P_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})})=\Sigma_{x}(I_{d}-P_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})}).

Moreover, at
M_{\star}
, we have
P_{\star}^{(\Sigma_{x})}=P_{\star}
is the orthogonal projection onto the span of
\mathrm{image}(M_{\star})
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}(Z)\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big(\Sigma_{x}^{-1}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}\Big)^{\top}Z^{\top}\Sigma_{x}^{-1}\,(I_{d}-P_{\star})\,\Sigma_{x}^{-1}Z\Big(\Sigma_{x}^{-1}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}\Big)

\displaystyle=\lVert(I_{d}-P_{\star})\,\Sigma_{x}^{-1}Z\Big(\Sigma_{x}^{-1}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}\Big)\rVert^{2}=\lVert(I_{d}-P_{\star})\,Z\Big(\Sigma_{x}^{-1}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}\Big)\rVert^{2}.

(G.14)

Since
\Sigma_{\star}^{-1}
acts as identity on
I-P_{\star}
. Now, we plug
eq.

G.7
into
Appendix

G
to get

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}(Z)\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert(I_{d}-P_{\star})\,(P_{\star}G+GP_{\star}-P_{\star}GP_{\star})\Big(\Sigma_{x}^{-1}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}\Big)\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert(I_{d}-P_{\star})\,GP_{\star}\Sigma_{x}^{-1}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}\rVert^{2}.

(G.15)

Finally, we plug
Equation

G.8
into
Appendix

G

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}(Z)\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert(I_{d}-P_{\star})\,(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\Sigma_{x}^{1/2}(W+W^{\top})\Sigma_{x}^{1/2})P_{\star}\Sigma_{x}^{-1}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\lVert(I_{d}-P_{\star})\,(W+W^{\top})P_{\star}\Sigma_{x}^{-1/2}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}\rVert^{2}.

(G.16)

Let
M_{\star}=U_{\star}\begin{pmatrix}D&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}U_{\star}^{\top}
with
D=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})
and

U_{\star}=[U_{1}\ U_{2}]
, where
U_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
spans
\operatorname{range}(M_{\star})
.
Then

P_{\star}=U_{1}U_{1}^{\top}=U_{\star}\begin{pmatrix}I_{k}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}U_{\star}^{\top},\qquad\Sigma_{x}=I_{d}+M_{\star}=U_{\star}\begin{pmatrix}I_{k}+D&0\\
0&I_{d-k}\end{pmatrix}U_{\star}^{\top},

so

\Sigma_{x}^{-1/2}=U_{\star}\begin{pmatrix}(I_{k}+D)^{-1/2}&0\\
0&I_{d-k}\end{pmatrix}U_{\star}^{\top}.

Rotate the standard Gaussian matrix by
\tilde{W}\coloneqq U_{\star}^{\top}WU_{\star}
(still i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
).
Then

(I_{d}-P_{\star})(W+W^{\top})P_{\star}\Sigma_{x}^{-1/2}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}=U_{\star}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
(\tilde{W}_{21}+\tilde{W}_{12}^{\top})&0\end{pmatrix}U_{\star}^{\top}\,U_{\star}\begin{pmatrix}(I_{k}+D)^{-1/2}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}U_{\star}^{\top}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}.

Since
A_{\star}\beta_{\star}\in\operatorname{range}(U_{1})
, letting
a\coloneqq U_{1}^{\top}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
gives

(I_{d}-P_{\star})(W+W^{\top})P_{\star}\Sigma_{x}^{-1/2}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}=U_{2}\,(\tilde{W}_{21}+\tilde{W}_{12}^{\top})\,(I_{k}+D)^{-1/2}\,a.

Because
U_{2}
is orthonormal,
\|U_{2}v\|_{2}=\|v\|_{2}
, hence the original quantity simplifies to

\frac{1}{2}\big\|(I_{d}-P_{\star})(W+W^{\top})P_{\star}\Sigma_{x}^{-1/2}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}\big\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\big\|(\tilde{W}_{21}+\tilde{W}_{12}^{\top})\,(I_{k}+D)^{-1/2}\,a\big\|_{2}^{2},\qquad a=U_{1}^{\top}A_{\star}\beta_{\star}.

In particular, since
\tilde{W}_{21}
has i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries and

\tilde{W}_{12}
is independent with the same law, the matrix

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\tilde{W}_{21}+\tilde{W}_{12}^{\top})
has i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries.
Therefore

\frac{1}{2}\big\|(\tilde{W}_{21}+\tilde{W}_{12}^{\top})\,(I_{k}+D)^{-1/2}\,a\big\|_{2}^{2}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\ \big\|\Xi\,(I_{k}+D)^{-1/2}\,U_{1}^{\top}A_{\star}\,\beta_{\star}\big\|_{2}^{2},\qquad\Xi_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1).

Equivalently, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}(Z)\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\ \|(I_{k}+D)^{-1/2}\,U_{1}^{\top}A_{\star}\,\beta_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\,\chi^{2}_{d-k}.

(G.17)

Appendix H
Proofs of
Section

6.3

This appendix verifies that the Gaussian-mixture example from
Section

6.3
satisfies the hypotheses of
Theorem

5.3
, and hence yields Corollary
6.6
.
The verification is modular:
(i) local-uniform moment bounds for the feature map,
(ii) rank stability/eigengap for the population feature second-moment
\Sigma(M)
,
(iii) a manifold CLT for the (pretraining) descriptor estimator
\hat{M}_{m}=R(\hat{U}_{m})
via an
M
-estimation CLT and a delta method,
and (iv) verification of the hypotheses of the general projector differentiability result proved in
Section

E.2
.

Throughout, we work on the regular set from
6.5
and write
M_{\star}\coloneqq R(U^{\star})
.

H.1
Model, features, and the quotient-level descriptor

Unlabeled distribution.

Let
K\geqslant 2
and
d\geqslant 1
. The unlabeled distribution is the spherical Gaussian mixture

X\mid(Z=i)\sim\mathcal{N}(u_{i}^{\star},I_{d}),\qquad Z\sim\mathrm{Unif}([K]),

with unknown centers
U^{\star}=(u_{1}^{\star},\dots,u_{K}^{\star})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}
.

Centered-mean subspace.

Define the empirical mean and centered second-moment matrix

\bar{u}(U):=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{K}u_{i},\qquad S(U):=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\big(u_{i}-\bar{u}(U)\big)\big(u_{i}-\bar{u}(U)\big)^{\top}.

Let
r_{\star}:=r(U^{\star})
and define
P_{U}
to be the orthogonal projector onto the leading
r_{\star}
-dimensional eigenspace of
S(U)

(on the regular neighborhood where this eigenspace is well-defined).
Write
P_{\star}:=P_{U^{\star}}
and
V_{\star}:=\mathrm{Im}(P_{\star})
.

Subspace-aware responsibilities.

For
i\in[K]
, define

\pi_{i}(x;U)=\frac{\exp\!\big(\langle P_{U}u_{i},P_{U}x\rangle-\tfrac{1}{2}\|P_{U}u_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\big)}{\sum_{j=1}^{K}\exp\!\big(\langle P_{U}u_{j},P_{U}x\rangle-\tfrac{1}{2}\|P_{U}u_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\big)}.

These satisfy
0\leqslant\pi_{i}\leqslant 1
and
\sum_{i=1}^{K}\pi_{i}=1
.

Feature map and hypothesis class.

The induced feature map has dimension
p(U)=K(r(U)+1)
:

\psi_{U}(x)=\Big(\pi_{1}(x;U)\big(P_{U}x-P_{U}u_{1}\big),\,\pi_{1}(x;U),\dots,\pi_{K}(x;U)\big(P_{U}x-P_{U}u_{K}\big),\,\pi_{K}(x;U)\Big).

Let
\mathcal{H}_{U}=\{\langle\theta,\psi_{U}(\cdot)\rangle:\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{p(U)}\}
and
\Pi_{U}:L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})\to L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{down}})
be the orthogonal projector onto
\mathcal{H}_{U}
.

Group action and quotient parameter.

Fix
U=(u_{1},\dots,u_{K})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}
.
The unlabeled mixture likelihood is invariant under relabeling of mixture components.
Accordingly, we consider the action of the permutation group
S_{K}
on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}
defined by

(g\cdot U)_{i}:=u_{g^{-1}(i)},\qquad g\in S_{K}.

We write

\underline{U}:=[U]\;\in\;(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}/S_{K}

for the equivalence class (orbit) of
U
under this action, and refer to
\underline{U}
as the

quotient-level parameter
.

Regular regime and local lifts.

Throughout this appendix we restrict attention to the regular regime in which the centers are
distinct:

u_{i}\neq u_{j}\qquad\text{for all }i\neq j.

In this regime the action of
S_{K}
is free, and the quotient
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}/S_{K}
is a smooth manifold of
dimension
Kd
. In particular, for any fixed
\underline{U}^{\star}=[U^{\star}]
there exists a
neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
and a smooth local section

s:\mathcal{U}\to(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K},\qquad s(\underline{U})\in\underline{U},

which amounts to choosing a deterministic ordering of the centers in a neighborhood of
U^{\star}
.
All constructions below are independent of the specific choice of section.

Permutation-invariant geometric quantities.

Both
\bar{u}(U)
and
P_{U}
are invariant under the action of
S_{K}
, and therefore depend only on the
quotient parameter
\underline{U}
. We may thus unambiguously write
\bar{u}(\underline{U})
and

P_{\underline{U}}
.

Responsibilities and equivariant feature map.

For a representative
U\in\underline{U}
, define the projected responsibilities

\pi_{i}(x;U):=\frac{\exp\!\big(\langle P_{U}u_{i},\;P_{U}x\rangle-\tfrac{1}{2}\|P_{U}u_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\big)}{\sum_{j=1}^{K}\exp\!\big(\langle P_{U}u_{j},\;P_{U}x\rangle-\tfrac{1}{2}\|P_{U}u_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\big)},\qquad i\in[K].

Let
\psi_{U}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{K(r_{\star}+1)}
denote the block-structured feature map

\psi_{U}(x)=\Big(\pi_{1}(x;U)(P_{U}x-P_{U}u_{1}),\,\pi_{1}(x;U);\;\dots;\;\pi_{K}(x;U)(P_{U}x-P_{U}u_{K}),\,\pi_{K}(x;U)\Big).

For any
g\in S_{K}
, let
\rho(g)
denote the block-permutation matrix acting on

\mathbb{R}^{K(r_{\star}+1)}
. A direct computation shows that the feature map is
S_{K}
-equivariant:

\psi_{g\cdot U}(x)=\rho(g)\,\psi_{U}(x).

To be more precise,
\rho(G)
consists of those permutations in
S_{2K}
that relabel
\pi_{i}(x,U)
and
\pi_{i}(x;U)(P_{U}x-P_{U}u_{i})
two the same label. Therefore, we can define a feature map
\phi(\cdot,\underline{U})
on the quotient manifold that satisfies all the assumptions of
Section

C.2
.

H.2
Local-uniform moment bounds for
\psi(\cdot,\underline{U})

We verify the local-uniform moment bounds from
E.2
for this model (with

X\sim\mu_{\mathrm{down}}
equal to the Gaussian mixture described in the example).

Lemma H.1

(Local-uniform polynomial moments of the Gaussian-mixture features(
E.2
)
.

Fix a compact neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
U^{\star}
in
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}
. Then for every
q\geqslant 1
,

\sup_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\ \mathbb{E}\big[\|\psi_{U}(X)\|^{q}\big]<\infty.

Consequently, for every neighborhood
\underline{\mathcal{U}}
of
\underline{U}_{\star}=[U^{\star}]
contained in the quotient image

[\mathcal{U}]\subset(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}/S_{K}
and every
q\geqslant 1

\sup_{\underline{U}\in\underline{\mathcal{U}}}\ \mathbb{E}\big[\|\psi_{s(\underline{U})}(X)\|^{q}\big]<\infty,

where
s:\underline{\mathcal{U}}\to(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}
is any local section (lift) with
s(\underline{U})\in\underline{U}
.

Proof.

Fix
U\in\mathcal{U}
. Since
0\leqslant\pi_{i}(x;U)\leqslant 1
and
\|P_{U}x\|_{2}\leqslant\|x\|_{2}
, we have for each
i

\big\|\pi_{i}(X;U)\big(P_{U}X-P_{U}u_{i}\big)\big\|_{2}\leqslant\|X\|_{2}+\|u_{i}\|_{2},\qquad|\pi_{i}(X;U)|\leqslant 1.

Thus there exists a constant
C<\infty
depending only on
K
such that

\|\psi_{U}(X)\|\leqslant C\Big(1+\|X\|_{2}+\max_{i\in[K]}\|u_{i}\|_{2}\Big).

Taking
q
-th moments and using
\sup_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\max_{i}\|u_{i}\|_{2}<\infty
, it remains to note that all polynomial moments of
X

are finite under a spherical Gaussian mixture, giving the stated uniform bound.

For the quotient-level statement, let
\underline{\mathcal{U}}\subset[\mathcal{U}]
and let
s
be a local section on

\underline{\mathcal{U}}
. Then
s(\underline{U})\in\mathcal{U}
for all
\underline{U}\in\underline{\mathcal{U}}
, so the same bound
applies uniformly to
\psi_{s(\underline{U})}(X)
.
∎

H.3
Rank stability and eigengap for the downstream second moment

Define the population second moment (feature covariance)

\Sigma(\underline{U})\;\coloneqq\;\mathbb{E}\big[\phi_{\underline{U}}(X)\phi_{\underline{U}}(X)^{\top}\big]\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p},

where
p
is the feature dimension. To invoke
Theorem

5.3
, we need rank stability and an eigengap at
0
for
\Sigma(\underline{U})
on a neighborhood of

\underline{U}_{\star}
. We reduce this to (a) continuity of
\underline{U}\mapsto\Sigma(\underline{U})
and (b) an eigengap at
\underline{U}_{\star}
.

Lemma H.2

(Continuity of
\underline{U}\mapsto\Sigma(\underline{U})
in operator norm)
.

Let
\underline{\mathcal{U}}
be a comapct neighborhood of
\underline{U}_{\star}
such that the conclusion of
Lemma

H.1
holds on

s(\underline{\mathcal{U}})
for some exponent
4+\delta
. Then
\underline{U}\mapsto\Sigma(\underline{U})
is continuous at
\underline{U}_{\star}
in operator norm.

Proof.

Fix
\underline{U}\in\underline{\mathcal{U}}
and write
Y_{\underline{U}}\coloneqq\phi_{\underline{U}}(X)\phi_{\underline{U}}(X)^{\top}
.
By
Lemma

H.1
(with exponent
1+\delta/4
) and Cauchy–Schwarz,

\sup_{\underline{U}\in\underline{\mathcal{U}}}\mathbb{E}\big[\|Y_{\underline{U}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{1+\delta/4}\big]\;\leqslant\;\sup_{\underline{U}\in\underline{\mathcal{U}}}\mathbb{E}\big[\|\phi_{\underline{U}}(X)\|^{2+\delta/2}\big]\;<\;\infty,

so
\{\|Y_{\underline{U}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}:\underline{U}\in\underline{\mathcal{U}}\}
is uniformly integrable.
Since
\phi_{\underline{U}}
is a continuous function of
\underline{U}
in
\underline{\mathcal{U}}
,
Y_{\underline{U}}\to Y_{\underline{U}_{\star}}
almost surely as
\underline{U}\to\underline{U}_{\star}
.
Uniform integrability then yields

\big\|\Sigma(\underline{U})-\Sigma(\underline{U}_{\star})\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\Big\|\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{\underline{U}}-Y_{\underline{U}_{\star}}\big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\;\leqslant\;\mathbb{E}\big[\|Y_{\underline{U}}-Y_{\underline{U}_{\star}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\big]\;\to\;0,

proving operator-norm continuity at
\underline{U}_{\star}
.
∎

Lemma H.3

(Local uniform invertibility from full rank at
\underline{U}_{\star}
)
.

Assume
\Sigma(\underline{U}_{\star})\succ 0
.
If
\underline{U}\mapsto\Sigma(\underline{U})
is continuous at
\underline{U}_{\star}
in operator norm
(e.g. by
Lemma

H.2
), then there exist a neighborhood
\underline{\mathcal{U}}
of
\underline{U}_{\star}

and a constant
\kappa>0
such that for all
\underline{U}\in\underline{\mathcal{U}}
,

\lambda_{\min}\!\big(\Sigma(\underline{U})\big)\geqslant\kappa.

In particular,
\Sigma(\underline{U})
is invertible on
\underline{\mathcal{U}}
and
\Sigma(\underline{U})^{+}=\Sigma(\underline{U})^{-1}
.

Proof.

Since
\Sigma(\underline{U}_{\star})\succ 0
, we have
\lambda_{\min}(\Sigma(\underline{U}_{\star}))>0
.
Let
\kappa:=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\lambda_{\min}(\Sigma(\underline{U}_{\star}))
.
By continuity in operator norm, for
\underline{U}
close enough to
\underline{U}_{\star}
we have

\|\Sigma(\underline{U})-\Sigma(\underline{U}_{\star})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\kappa
.
Weyl’s inequality then yields

\lambda_{\min}\!\big(\Sigma(\underline{U})\big)\geqslant\lambda_{\min}\!\big(\Sigma(\underline{U}_{\star})\big)-\|\Sigma(\underline{U})-\Sigma(\underline{U}_{\star})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geqslant 2\kappa-\kappa=\kappa,

proving the claim.
∎

H.4
Pretraining CLT for the quotient estimator
\hat{\underline{U}}_{m}=[\hat{U}_{m}]

We now establish the pretraining fluctuation input required by
Section

E.2
:
a tangent-space CLT for the quotient-level estimator
\hat{\underline{U}}_{m}
in normal coordinates around

\underline{U}_{\star}=[U^{\star}]
.

Pretraining estimator.

For concreteness, let
\hat{U}_{m}
be a (measurable) local minimizer of the empirical negative log-likelihood
for the mixture with equal weights and known covariance:

\ell(U;x)=-\log\Big(\frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\|x-u_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\big)\Big),\qquad\hat{U}_{m}\in\argmin_{U}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ell(U;X_{j}).

Define the quotient estimator
\hat{\underline{U}}_{m}\coloneqq[\hat{U}_{m}]\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}/S_{K}
.

Lemma H.4

(Score and Hessian for the spherical equal-weight MoG likelihood)
.

Let

p_{U}(x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\varphi_{d}(x-u_{i}),\qquad\varphi_{d}(t)\coloneqq(2\pi)^{-d/2}\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\|t\|_{2}^{2}\Big),\qquad\ell(U;x)\coloneqq-\log p_{U}(x).
